and i think the first conclusion is that we have to have this debate and we have to continue it. that's the goal of the memory and consciousness platform. why is such a platform important at european level? it's important as a tool against forgetting the past. if we forget the past, of course, it's an opportunity to return to us in the future through the back door. it's also an opportunity to fight the relativisation of past crimes. hitler and stalin are the same blood. of course, there were countries where nazism enabled or helped to bring about criminal communism. but any kind of relativism -- and i don't want to get into the politics of it -- any kind of relativism is extremely dangerous. secondly, we must maintain awareness of history as part of european education. and here we think, and this is after all part of one of the conclusions of the hearings that took place last week, that we need to strengthen the financial instruments to have in the field of education an awareness of the totalitarian past on the european continent. we need the means to do this and i would like to thank the commission and commissioner figeľ in particular for the commission's willingness to cooperate on this matter. and last but not least, what the debate should be about. the presidency is a short thing. it takes six months. we are halfway through, and i am certainly under no illusion that if this parliament is about to dissolve we can do the impossible, but i think the idea of creating a kind of pan-european institution, whether at the level of a museum or a research institute or a foundation, is what we need. the hearing last week brought together representatives of some of the similar institutions that exist at national level, and i think the need for such an institution at european level is more than desirable. but of course this is a task for institutions which guarantee some further continuity rather than a six-month rotating presidency. if our task was to help to initiate a debate, i thank you once again for making it a success, and i invite those of you who will be the guarantors of that continuity to continue the debate, perhaps one day it will lead to the fact that such an institution will actually exist.
member of the commission. - i have already stated the essence of the commission's message at the outset and can only reaffirm not only the readiness but also the will, which was also one of the points. if we are to have the will of the european commission to help this process, i have already mentioned the next steps, which is to complete a study of the different methods, mechanisms of the member states in this area and to submit the expected report next year. which i do want to add a little to the margo debate. some have suggested that the 20th anniversary of the fall of communism, the fall of the berlin wall, the iron curtain in europe, is a long time. i think it is never too late, and it would be too late if this parliament, the new enlarged parliament, had not spoken out against the crimes and totalitarian communism, or rather had not opened this debate, because many people have come with this experience and most of the debaters were from the new member states. i too have lived most of my life in the totalitarian system of czechoslovakia, and it is up to the personal and collective experience of all of us to build together a european memory, a european community, and at the same time to help the processes that prevent a return, or as it were, to the trivialization and rejection of crimes, the rejection of the truth. the denial of these crimes is actually a relativisation not only of the truth, but of ethics, of morality, and that is then the way to more and more problems and the liquidation of man by the quasi-elimination of the problem. it was one of the principles that man was eliminated from the difficulties, under the stalin regime. i see the enlarged union as more complete, and that is why it has the ability to name what has been perceived as different forms of totalitarianism, in different countries, different forms. and we are responsible for naming them, for remembering them and taking appropriate action, whether in terms of restitution, rehabilitation, the establishment of justice and the maintenance of the rule of law, respect for human dignity and all that freedom and democracy mean. i also want to say that we often feel as if the threats come from far outside, and that the dividing line of the world today is not religions, or any other civilizational and cultural dividing line, but a line that speaks of respect for life, respect for the human being on the one hand (what we define as human rights), and on the other fanaticism of any kind. and there were many, they're different. we know them in the world today, and that's why the education that alexander vondra mentioned is very important, so that young people and less young people can distinguish between respect for the person and extremism, populism, nationalism, fanaticism, different, different expressions. i want to conclude by saying that the european commission is really supporting this process, and we will be looking for ways to help. it's just one step in this debate, but other issues. much more needs to be done at member state level too. i would like to conclude by saying that the fact that the union can be enlarged today is due to the sacrifices of many, that it is not the result of an automatic process. neither the common europe, nor democracy, nor freedom, nor the rule of law is a given automatic state, but the result of a huge effort, often bloody, and this must be remembered. and i would like to conclude with what i think mr tunne kelam said about the enlargement of the union, not just the geography and the market and the number of countries, but the enlargement of conscience, the enlargement of memory, the enlargement of respect and responsibility. if this is part of the process that we are celebrating now, five years after enlargement, then we are ready for other processes, for another future. if we're missing that, the journey will be much more difficult. thank you very much and i wish you an expansion not only of memory but also of responsibility.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place at the next part-session. the court of justice shall have jurisdiction to hear and determine the appeal.
the rapporteur. - the mr president, we live in an age where everyone - governments, private companies, even criminals - want to have as much access as possible to our electronic data, to our private lives. the internet in particular offers details of our private lives that would have been unthinkable a few years ago. at the same time, it is clear that it is jeopardizing the exercise of our basic fundamental rights: freedom of speech, freedom of political action, freedom of knowledge and education, freedom of association. less clear is that we risk violating exactly these same freedoms by using the internet. secret surveillance by governments, private companies, or even criminals, of what we do or see online. and even less clear is how we can therefore find the balance here. how can we regulate the internet in such a way that it allows us to derive its benefits while limiting its obvious risks? the report attempts to answer these questions. among other things: - firstly, calls for a european initiative to create a global charter of fundamental rights on the internet; - secondly, points to the need to combat old and new crimes on the internet, such as identity theft and copyright protection, effectively and proportionately, while stressing that no legislation can result in the systematic surveillance of all citizens, suspected or not, innocent or not, because this would of course constitute a flagrant violation of privacy; - thirdly, on the right of citizens to access the internet, calls on governments to ensure that it is available even to the poorest citizens, even in the most remote areas; - fourthly, stresses that computer literacy in the 21st century is the new illiteracy, as was illiteracy in the 20th century, and that access to the internet is therefore a corresponding fundamental right to access to education; - fifth, it calls for measures to restrict user consent, a major issue which i will discuss below. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. let me give you an example: a few decades ago, no one knew what newspaper i read; just my family and maybe some friends. that is why, even in dictatorial regimes, the secret services were eager to find out, to 'sweep me down'. they say that labrinides reads this newspaper, so he must be a communist, or he must be an american. today, every newspaper i read leaves a mark on me. that means that private companies can do the same thing, create a profile of me, of my political beliefs, of my dietary habits, even of my health. does the fact that i visit these websites mean that i have consented to take my society back 40 years? the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment and the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very important one. this balance seems difficult, but it's not. it is possible. we have to stop treating cyberspace as something that is outside of our everyday lives, something special. because it's our life. that means, mr. president, that whatever rights and whatever barriers exist for police and private companies on the internet, they must also exist off the internet. otherwise we risk abolishing freedoms to find security and, ultimately, not having any freedoms and not having any real security. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs of all the political groups i see here in this chamber for their extremely important support. i thank all the members of the european parliament in the committee on civil liberties and i look forward to the adoption of this report by the house.
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank not only parliament in general but especially mr stavros lambrinidis for his important report, which provides very timely input to the promotion of fundamental freedoms and security on the internet. whilst the internet has acquired a stronger and stronger weight in modern societies and economies, impacting in many areas of our lives, the tremendous pace of technological development raises, at the same time, significant challenges which need to be duly addressed if we want the full opportunities of the internet and the information society to unravel. we particularly share mr lambrinidis’s concerns on the protection of personal data, an issue which is of utmost relevance to internet users. let me assure you that the commission continues to be committed to strengthening citizens’ fundamental rights and fundamental freedoms, and in particular to ensuring a high level of protection of privacy and personal data, both on the internet and in other contexts. i firmly believe that the pursuit of adequate privacy protection does not stand in opposition to the need to ensure greater security. indeed, these two objectives can and should be pursued in synergy. the stability and security of the internet were our priorities during the world summit on the information society in 2005, and we are continuing with these objectives. these issues and will shortly be presented through a new strategy for the protection of critical information infrastructures and in order to enhance europe’s preparation against large-scale cyber attacks and disruptions. this strategy includes an action plan which defines a road map to promote principles and guidelines for the stability and resilience of the internet. strategic cooperation with third countries will be developed within the strategy, notably in information society dialogues, as a vehicle to build global consensus in this area. at the same time, the commission is convinced that it is necessary to ensure respect for fundamental freedoms such as freedom of expression on the internet. again, these two goals are not mutually exclusive. your report further elaborates on the possibility of working on global standards, data protection and freedom of speech. the commission participates in the yearly international conferences of data protection commissioners and follows the ongoing work on possible future international standards on privacy and personal data protection. we are strongly committed to promoting the high standards of protection which eu citizens enjoy at present. regarding freedom of speech, the commission will continue to promote this fundamental right in international forums. new legislation in this area would not be considered as the way forward for the time being. we already have a number of binding international instruments on this subject. at this point in time, i believe that it would be useful to engage in effective reflection on the proper ways to enforce the existing legislation. so it is about implementation. this reflection should also include and help global commercial players to better define their roles and their responsibilities in the promotion and strengthening of the fundamental freedom of expression in the global online environment of the internet. let me conclude with a general comment. i believe we should tackle the serious challenges that this report brings to light and make sure that the concrete exercise of rights and freedoms is not unduly limited on the internet. for example, an essential element of the commission’s strategy for a secure information society, since 2006, has been to have a holistic approach, ensuring coordination amongst stakeholders but also recognising that each of them has specific roles and responsibilities to fulfil. we all have responsibilities to make sure that our actions on the internet do not unduly limit – and, if possible, that they promote – the security of others on this medium. it is, therefore, in the spirit of cooperation that the commission welcomes and supports this report.
the committee on culture and education has adopted an opinion on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of children against the dangers arising from the use of certain dangerous substances. - the mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr stavros lambrinidis, on his important report and on his commitment to the protection of personal data. a principle that most of us respect, and i personally do. as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on culture and education, i believe that the internet is an excellent platform for the dissemination of culture and knowledge; i would like to thank the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done. examples, such as digitized museum archives, e-books, music, audio-visual material, can be accessed by people in every corner of the earth. but in the vastness of the internet and the world, cultural material is unfortunately not adequately protected. piracy tends to be the rule rather than the exception, and it is creators who are harmed by the illegal trafficking of their intellectual property. in other words: poets, songwriters, musicians, composers, producers and, in general, every member of the creative community. three of the reasons that help to develop piracy are: technological ease and the low cost of illegal copying, adverse economic conditions and the spread of the internet. amendment no 4 reiterates the recommendation of the committee on culture to strike the right balance between the rights and freedoms of all concerned, and to ensure and protect also all the fundamental rights of individuals under the charter of fundamental rights of the european union in the context of the widespread use of the internet. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of the child are respected and that the rights of the child are protected.
mr president, it has become almost a habit in this house to consider a report on the internet. this is welcome because it allows the member states and the european union to finally deal effectively with these topical issues concerning the use of the internet. i congratulate mr lambrinidis on his acceptance of the amendments tabled by his colleagues in his very extensive report, and on his enrichment of the subject under discussion by introducing several new aspects. the last time we were in parliament we discussed the issues of online video games and the risks to minors, and the european union's financial mechanism for a safe internet for minors. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the internet is not used for illegal purposes. the report will therefore serve as a catalogue to be examined in depth by legal experts, who will then carry out the research project that will provide the various actors mentioned in the report with the tools to draft legislation that will enshrine the rule of law in the democratic use of the internet. and we certainly cannot talk about democracy when every citizen does not have the right, regardless of their financial capacity, to use the internet. that is not the reality today, but we hope that it will be one day.
member of the commission. − mr president, i should like to thank all the speakers for their committed and interesting contributions. i just want to add two points about what i said at the beginning. we share, for example, concerns on intellectual property rights and the balanced approach which is needed here. this is important for the overall evolution, or development, of the information society. enforcement of these property rights must be duly balanced with fundamental freedoms or rights, which are enumerated in the report, including the right to privacy, protection of personal data and the right to participate in the information society. many of you mentioned children – minors – protection of those who are probably most facing these challenges, and they are sitting at computers daily. rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank the commission. to make the interpreters’ lives easier – as i am speaking off text – i will, as an exception, do this in english. i respect the sensitivities of those who are concerned about the protection of intellectual property. but i have the impression that they are fighting a battle on the wrong report. my report is not a single-issue report; it is a report that talks generally about protecting fundamental rights and security on the internet and, indeed, that is why it received unanimous support. however, to the extent that it does refer to intellectual property rights, one would think by listening to some of the speeches that it ignores them. let me read, in particular, how balanced we have attempted to be with this report. in paragraph 1(k), we urge the to council to ‘proceed to the adoption of the directive on criminal measures aimed at the enforcement of intellectual property rights, following an assessment, in the light of contemporary innovation research, of the extent to which it is necessary and proportionate’. this is what the report states. the amendments, however, are anything but balanced. amendments that delete what the report calls for – which is a prohibition to the systematic surveillance of all users, whether or not suspect and whether or not guilty, to protect any security right – are anything but balanced. they call on us to totally abandon fundamental rights to protect something else. secondly, amendments that delete or water down a very precise and specific reference in the report – that controversial political speech should not be criminalised – are amendments that i oppose, and i am glad to hear that many others in this room do as well. political speech has to be protected, especially when it is controversial. if everyone in this room agreed with each other, we would not need freedom of speech legislation. it is when we do not – and especially to protect the speech that may particularly be angering to people like me or others – that we have those laws. this reference in the report does not talk about ‘criminal’ speech. it talks specifically about ‘controversial political’ speech. therefore, i urge everyone to support this particular paragraph in the report and to support the report in general. i am deeply grateful to everyone – even those who disagree with me – who was here tonight. i know it is not easy. thank you for your support during all these months leading up to this report. i look forward to working with you on your reports in the future and showing you similar understanding and support.
i'm the editor. - the mr president, commissioner, the informal meeting of sports ministers in biarritz in november 2008 was the first substantive european sports forum at the level of transnational cooperation. it is a first, substantial and important step. principles such as subsidiarity, autonomy and self-regulation in sport are widely applied at national and regional level. they cannot, however, answer the fundamental question of whether, in the end, sport has a social role or a purely commercial dimension which inevitably transforms a sporting activity into a product of the internal market. today, the intense commercialisation of sport, combined with its opening up to purely commercial sectors such as media advertising, has given it purely economic characteristics. the court of justice of the european union has already been called on several occasions to determine - in specific cases - whether a sporting activity is a purely public service or whether, on the contrary, it also has economic implications which are inherent in a service of general economic interest. the specific social role of sport, although it does not justify its general exemption from the rule of community law, is still recognised and allows for restrictions, provided that such exceptions are justified by the social role of sport itself. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. - first, the same problem of the autonomy of the organizations; the autonomy of sports clubs and federations should be guaranteed. however, there are clear cases where self-regulation does not ensure equal treatment of all operators; - secondly, there is an urgent need for the commission to give us the necessary guidance now on the question of the definition of the concept of a service of general economic interest in the field of sport and the criteria that limit the enforcement of the internal market freedoms and the rules of competition; and - thirdly, a matter which may not be mentioned in the oral question but is of great importance is what has emerged at national level, namely the way in which the television rights for sporting events are managed by public or non-public broadcasters. the entry of many new owners of providers, both new technology and telecommunications-based, has been the main enticement for many sports federations to sell these rights in preference. however, it should be stressed at this point that, precisely because of the social role of sport, it would be wrong to promote a system which lacks solidarity between clubs and in which economic competition is enhanced. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment in sport is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done in this area. the role and organisation of sports clubs is an important issue in the way they negotiate with the european commission, the committee on culture and the european parliament.
member of the commission. − mr president, i welcome the statement by the eu sports ministers at their meeting last november in biarritz. they are important in driving the political debate on sport forward step by step and also in strengthening dialogue structures on sport, as was recommended in our white paper in 2007. this white paper on sport is making good progress in implementation. the recent european council declaration on sport, in december, is also a very important step forward and calls for strengthened dialogue with the ioc and the sport movement as a priority. the commission has moved swiftly to follow up both calls from the political side. however, dialogue in sport remains a challenging task, given the diversity of sport and the complexity of the sport structures. in january, in lausanne, i met with the ioc president, mr jacques rogge, and representatives of international sport federations. the meeting confirmed that sport organisations are really moving away from insisting on general exemptions – ‘block exemptions’ – from the ‘acquis communautaire’ for sport, and are ready to address sport-related topics with legal implications on a theme-per-theme basis. i think this is the appropriate way to achieve more clarity regarding the application of european union law to sport, in addition to guidance provided in the white paper. we are also exchanging views with team sports. my services met last week with the main international and european team sport federations to discuss free movement of athletes and non-discrimination aspects in sport. i intend to follow up on these discussions by convening a further meeting at this high level in june. finally, good progress has been achieved in setting up eu social dialogue structures in sport. it started in football and i believe it will continue in other sectors. regarding the shape of the dialogue framework, there simply cannot be a single, formal framework for consultation with the sport movement. given the specific organisation of sport, the dialogue must take account of the different levels of sport. i am committed to a constructive partnership with all actors in sport respecting the autonomy of sport, subsidiarity principles and national and eu legal frameworks. i think this is crucial in shaping the future eu policy framework for sport, which – without prejudging developments regarding the lisbon treaty – we might have to implement as early as next year. here i also would like to thank the european parliament, and especially the committee on culture and education, for its support. you also, notably, approved the specific line, or budget, for a preparatory action, which we will soon start to implement. this is an important opportunity and will help us to frame our ideas for the way ahead.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as rapporteur for the white paper on sport, i had the opportunity to present the european parliament's position at the meeting of the ministers for sport of the member states of the european union in biarritz. the white paper stresses the need to respect the autonomy and specificity of sport. the political debate on sport in europe often places great importance on the so-called 'european sport model'. during the discussions with the european sports bodies on the white paper on sport, the commission and the european parliament agreed that the specific nature of sport must be protected in all circumstances. there are two approaches to the specificity of european sport: - first, the specific nature of sporting activities and sporting rules; and - second, the specific nature of the structure of sport. as regards the latter, the european union recognises the autonomy of sports organisations and representative structures such as organisers of professional leagues. however, in order to achieve better and more effective coordination, there should be a minimum of regulation at european level. the commission and the european sports community therefore believe that most of the challenges can be met by self-regulation, while respecting the principles of good administration and respecting community law. in general, ladies and gentlemen, mr commissioner, mr president, the european union is very careful in its approach to and regulation of sports matters, provided that they are compatible with european law.

rapporteur. − mr president, i am grateful to everyone for their support. there is just one slight point of confusion for me. the oral amendment is on the voting list but nobody stood up to support it. does that mean that it fell and did not come to the vote at all? is that correct?
in writing. the report makes repeated, well-known observations on the monopoly situation that has been created in the processing and marketing of food. it does not, however, state that this situation is a conscious choice of the eu and the governments of the member states in the context of the lisbon strategy and the cap. market liberalisation, merger and acquisition incentives and the pursuit of profit and competitiveness in the food industry have led to multinational dominance, higher consumer prices, lower producer prices and higher capital gains. the cap has made a decisive contribution to this process by abolishing minimum guaranteed prices, selling off the small and medium-sized farmer to the wto in order for multinationals to secure cheap raw materials, and persecuting or reducing cooperatives despite the hypocritical proclamations of the parties of the euro-road. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the milk market is not affected by the crisis. today it promotes the consumption of products of reduced nutritional value, leaving thousands of farmers exposed who cannot sell their produce in a country which produces 50% of its needs. the struggle for cheap and safe food, for the survival of the poor peasantry, requires a strong alliance of workers, farmers, self-employed, popular power and popular economy.
in writing. the 'high expectations' of the european parliament from the election of obama to the us presidency are really only for the imperialists, who legitimately expect their interests to be served more effectively. the people must not entertain any illusions that there will be a change of policy in their favour. after all, the very proclamations and statements of the new president of the united states leave no room for doubt. the report calls for closer and deeper eu cooperation with the us and nato after this election. to this end, it proposes the creation of an institutional body for the coordination of the foreign and security policy of the two imperialist centres (eu-us) at an even higher level. european and american imperialists, in a period of sharpening of their rivalries and contradictions due to the capitalist economic crisis, are simultaneously trying to coordinate their cooperation to face the popular resistance. this is the aim of the call for a common and effective response to 'global challenges', 'defence and security issues' and 'regional issues'. the willingness expressed in the report to accept guantanamo detainees in the eu if the us so requests is characteristic. in the united attack of the eu, the us and nato against them, the peoples must oppose their own common front to overthrow the imperialist order of things
the next item is the debate on the oral question to the commission by mrs doris pack, on behalf of the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats, on the role of culture in the development of the european regions (o-0064/2009 - b6-0226/2009).
member of the commission. − madam president, allow me first of all to thank you for the opportunity, on behalf of commissioner figeľ, to address the place of culture in our policies and its particular contribution to the development of european regions and cities. the importance of culture is taken into consideration in different ways at community level. in the context of the eu’s cohesion policy, regional and local strategies have successfully integrated culture to support creativity and to promote innovation. cohesion policy supports, for instance, the protection of our cultural heritage, the development of cultural infrastructure and services, the development of regional attractiveness and its link with sustainable tourism, but also the regeneration of local economies and the development of cross-border strategies. in 2007, the commission launched the european agenda for culture, which is now in its first stages of implementation. this new strategic approach to culture sets common objectives and aims to propel the economic, social and political value of culture by strengthening its transversal role. in this framework, the commission and the member states are cooperating under a new open method of cooperation to strengthen joint efforts in areas with a direct impact on local and regional development strategies. this will, for instance, help maximise the potential of creative and cultural industries, in particular smes; promote access to culture, and encourage the mobility of cultural professionals. feeding into the ongoing reflection, the commission will soon launch an independent study on the contribution of culture to local and regional economic development as part of the european regional policy. the results of this study will help underline the value of investing in the cultural and creative sectors and will illustrate the links between such investment, specific regional development objectives and the lisbon agenda for growth and jobs. the study will also contribute to the preparation of a green paper on the potential of cultural and creative industries, which is currently under preparation and is due for adoption by the commission in early 2010. the commission regularly organises conferences with representatives from local and regional authorities. let me simply highlight the open days, which every year bring together in brussels a large number of stakeholders to debate a wide range of issues relating to regional and cohesion policy. aspects relating to culture have been regularly considered in the context of these workshops. moreover, within the context of other european policies, such as the eu’s integrated maritime policy, the commission also endeavours to involve civil society actors in highlighting europe’s rich maritime heritage. thus, next may, during the european maritime day celebrations in rome, stakeholders will examine the links between maritime heritage and sustainable regional tourism, amongst others. finally, i also wish to mention the european cultural forum, which will be organised for the first time by the commission, in the context of the european agenda for culture in brussels, on 29 and 30 september, and will bring together representatives of the cultural sector and national authorities, including local and regional authorities.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. madam president, commissioner, i should like to begin by congratulating you on the initiative on such an interesting subject as the development of the regions of the european union through culture. cultural heritage is an important element of the identity and historical development of the peoples of europe. the protection and preservation of the cultural heritage is therefore of particular importance for the education of the young generation and the respect of european identity. regardless of its european, national or local dimension, cultural heritage is a fundamental value for european citizens. we all find that the spotlight is on the big cities, where the most famous museums and monuments are located. however, it is a fact that the european countryside, which represents 90% of the european territory, is suffering from neglect and economic decline. european cultural programmes therefore contribute significantly to the development of economic activity in the regions. it is not just the supply of jobs and employment, it is the creation of cultural and historical tourist attractions that will contribute to the sustainable development of these areas. we therefore find that culture contributes directly to the development of the cultural education of europeans and indirectly to the economic well-being of the regions which need it most.
member of the commission. − madam president, i would like to thank the honourable members for the number of points that they have raised. i will certainly forward your points and the concerns that you have expressed to commissioner figeľ. i would, however, like to express some general points and reactions. mrs pack spoke about the harmonising of rules at european level and how this impacts on regional diversity. i would like to point out that having harmonisation at european level is necessary in order to ensure a level playing field across the european union so that its citizens can fully benefit from the single internal market. this would not, however, mean that such harmonisation leads to less cultural diversity. in fact this was also the outcome of the european year on intercultural dialogue. furthermore, the commission, through its regional policy, promotes cultural diversity and invests in culture, both directly and indirectly, indeed by involving regional authorities and stakeholders. in a number of policy areas the commission seeks to promote diversity and take account of regional specificities across the european union. concerning the point raised on the issue of culture and the economic crisis and the overall contribution to growth and jobs, let me just recall that the commission is undertaking a study this year to analyse how the cultural dimension has been integrated into the regional development strategies for 2007-2013. the results of the study will highlight the value of investing in the cultural sector, including cultural and creative industries, and will underline the links between such investment, the specific regional development objectives and the lisbon agenda. on the green paper i would like to inform you, as i said at the outset, that this policy document is due in the first quarter of 2010 and its aim is to launch an open consultation process. it has three main policy objectives. firstly, to advance a more strategic approach. secondly, to unlock the potential of european cultural and creative industries, and, thirdly, to contribute to the development of strategies aimed at encouraging better linkages between cultural and creative industries and other sectors of the economy, and thus to connect culture and creativity with innovation and the wider economy. the regional dimension will of course be fully taken into account in this context. i would like to conclude by referring to the statement made by mr posselt that culture is often destroyed by nationalisms. certainly not by the european union: it firmly believes in and upholds unity and diversity.

the rapporteur. - the i would like to thank my colleagues on the committee on regional development for the spirit of cooperation with which we have worked. in the wake of the financial crisis that has erupted in recent months, we all know that the commission, in order to strengthen the european economy and strengthen the central pillars of the lisbon strategy for growth and jobs, adopted the communication of 26 november 2008 on a european economic recovery programme for the member states and their regions. the programme encourages the member states to reprogram their structural funds operational programmes in the energy sector, with particular attention to improving the energy efficiency of buildings, given that the building sector is one of the industries which generates a large number of jobs. the 2006 general regulation (ec) no 1083/2006 on the structural funds therefore needed to be revised. in this context, and more specifically with regard to the energy efficiency of buildings, i have undertaken, as rapporteur for the european parliament, to revise the relevant regulation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the erdf has so far considered as eligible expenditure for housing, and in particular for energy efficiency and renewable energy in housing, only expenditure incurred by member states that joined the european union after may 2004. in my report i thought it appropriate, firstly, to focus all 27 member states on the framework for the revision of the regulation on facilitating energy efficiency and renewable energy sources in the housing sector. i think that this proposal is of great importance, since it is not based on the date of accession but on the economic situation of a state or region. i would like to point out here that there are significant problems of access to housing in many cities and regions of europe, not all of which are necessarily in the territory of a new member state. secondly, i would like to see a spending limit for these investments of 4% of the total erdf budget, to remove the reference to low-income households, which was included in the commission's original proposal, and leave it to the member states to determine the categories of housing that will be eligible. on this basis, i considered it crucial to assign to the member states the responsibility for the eligibility of the housing category, with the possibility for the member states to set specific criteria of interest, such as the financial situation of the owners, the geographical areas (island, mountainous, non-mountain, etc.). finally, the increase in the lump sums to eur 50 000 is significant because it reflects the cost data of our time. the report i have tabled is intended to express the european parliament's views on this subject and to represent the compromise reached with the council in the co-decision procedure on the changes we made to the original proposal. the revision of that regulation does not affect eligible housing costs and boosts the activity of important sectors of the economy such as the construction and renewable energy and energy system industries. more generally, it is in line with the principle of subsidiarity, since it provides support to the member states; it is in line with the principle of proportionality, since it applies to all member states; it promotes the objectives of cohesion policy as set out in article 158 of the treaty on european union; and it does not burden the community budget for the period 2007 to 2013, but it speeds up the payment of advances and interim payments. i would like to make it clear here how important it is that we have the addition of three additional forms of eligible expenditure: indirect expenditure, flat-rate expenditure and flat-rate expenditure. in conclusion, i would like to say that we have with us today commissioner barrot, representing the regional policy commissioner, mrs hübner, who will - as agreed - make a binding statement from the commission on the evaluation of the new measures for 2010, a statement which concerns all three regulations.

member of the commission. - the mr president, i should first like to thank and congratulate the rapporteurs, mrs mcavan and mr tatarella, for their excellent reports on the proposals for the revision of the european union eco-label and the eco-management and control system. these are two important environmental policy measures which form an integral part of the action plan for sustainable consumption and production. it is positive that agreement can be reached at first reading. the contribution of members of the european parliament was decisive; the commission has succeeded in keeping intact the environmental objective of the commission proposal, but has also set even more ambitious targets in a number of important areas. the agreement reached at first reading confirms the institutions' willingness to tackle urgently the problems posed by unsustainable consumption and production. the revision of the eco-management and audit scheme, known as emas, is enabling organisations and businesses around the world to manage the environmental impact of their activities more effectively. emas contributes to the continuous improvement of the environmental performance of organisations and businesses, including, of course, compliance with relevant environmental legislation. it also offers organisations and businesses additional benefits, not only through direct cost savings, but also through reduced reporting red tape and incentives from the competent authorities of the member states. the review of the system has enabled us to respond to the growing demand from consumers for objective, unbiased and reliable information on the environmental impact of the products they buy. the revised system will enable us to increase the variety of eco-labelled products on the market and encourage businesses to improve their environmental performance. the eco-label logo will also offer them many competitive advantages. for example: reducing fees, imposing stricter environmental standards, excluding dangerous substances, simplifying criteria and linking them to public procurement policy and other european union policies. the scope of the revised eco-label regulation is also extended. the regulation is becoming more flexible and able to respond to new environmental challenges and priorities. as a framework legislative act, the eco-label regulation does not set specific criteria for products. instead, it provides for the establishment of environmental criteria for selected product categories, with the aim of being able to award the logo to the best products per category. today, we are faced with a widespread distribution on the market of environmental signs, images, texts, which can cause confusion to the consumer. from images of forests on containers containing dangerous substances, to claims of carbon-neutral food or even green cars. so the consumer doesn't know who to trust. the compromise proposal for a regulation on the eco-label helps to remove the doubts. before criteria and product categories for food and drink are established, a study on the added value that the label can offer will be carried out. following this study and the adoption of a commission decision through the parliamentary examination procedure, the eco-label logo could be awarded to products with the best environmental performance. i hope that parliament will give its full support to this positive package of proposals. the eco-label is one of the few cases of real and direct communication between citizens and the european union on environmental issues. the eco-label helps citizens to make better purchasing decisions and, by extension, to be directly and actively involved in the fight against unsustainable consumption. the european commission is able to accept the compromise package of proposals as a whole in order to reach agreement at first reading on both regulations. i would like to thank the rapporteurs again for their excellent work.

in writing. the commission proposal for a regulation on a community code of conduct is part of the eu's measures to intensify repression against migrants, to create a 'fortress europe' for the peoples of third countries and for the peoples of the eu itself. the eu visa code sets uniform, stricter rules for all its member states for the issuing of visas to third-country nationals entering the eu. it requires biometric data (fingerprints and all ten fingers) to be included in visas even for children of 12 years old! this information, together with a number of other personal data, will be entered in the vis information system, which the eu has already established and which aims to become the largest single database of personal data - an open dossier - of all third country nationals. the european parliament's report does not change the substance, direction and logic of the visa code, which is yet another tool for monitoring and tougher repression of migrants, within the overall eu anti-migration policy as expressed in the migration pact. once again, it is clear that the eu is the enemy of the peoples, of migrants and refugees, and that it is holding them hostage to the savage exploitation of capital.

member of the commission. − madam president, i could not agree more with the rapporteur, whom i would like to congratulate on the swift and efficient work done. we are facing here an obstacle to freedom of movement because, if you punish citizens on their phone bill when they cross a border, that is frankly not the internal market we strive for and that we wish to construct. so in order to give this freedom of movement to our citizens we have to get rid of those obstacles. those obstacles concern a lot of people. there are roughly 150 million citizens in the european union who use their mobile phone at least once per year for roaming while they are abroad, and they have to pay the price. it is the students, the travellers and the holidaymakers, but it is also the cross-border workers, the journalists and the businesspeople who are handicapped because of very high charges for communication. that is why i thank the european parliament for having so swiftly responded to the commission’s proposal. i think we have managed to get a proposal put into practice in the shortest ever time in the european union – only seven months from proposal to implementation. that is a first of its kind, and it is a first of its kind in the interests of the european consumers. i would just like to say a few words about what the first roaming package has achieved. on voice roaming it has achieved a 60% lower price for the citizen and 30% more traffic for the industry. the industry has also – and this is interesting – seen a steady rise in the penetration rate of the mobile phones used by citizens. we now have an average penetration rate in europe of 119%. that is an absolute world record and, with the prices for roaming going down, that allows freedom to those citizens who utilise their mobile phone. it also gives good money to the industry because their traffic is rising continuously. at the same time, national prices are going down. since the barroso commission took things in hand, domestic prices for mobile communication have decreased by roughly 35%. that is as an answer to those who say that, if we bring down the cost of roaming, national prices go up. that is not the case. the statistics show just the contrary. now we are going a step further: firstly, of course, on voice roaming. i think it is very good that we are continuing to bring down the caps so that the competition of the offers can take place under the caps. it is very important that we add to this sms roaming, because every year in the european union 2.5 billion text messages are sent. the value of the revenue from this for the industry is roughly eur 800 million. now, who is sending the smss? mainly our young people: 77% of young people up to the age of 24 use text messages when they are abroad because it is easier and cheaper for them. so they are punished when they have to pay an extraordinarily high price with regard to the cost to the operator when they are roaming. good: we are bringing those prices down so that text messaging will become a normal thing if you are at home or if you are in a neighbouring country. so consumers will greatly benefit from this. the reduction – which will be voted by parliament tomorrow – means 60% for text messaging. concerning the per-second billing for roaming calls: simply said, do not make the people pay for what they have not utilised, only make them pay for what they have really utilised. we will also bring today’s hidden charge of 24% down, so that you pay only for what you have really consumed. and for data roaming, where we believe that this is a future development. but, whilst we want to have a future development so that you can download, wherever you are, a film, a newspaper article or a photograph to send to your friends, today you have shocking bills. i have received copies of bills where people have been in another country for three or four days and have to pay several thousands of euros just because they have downloaded their favourite television show or newspaper articles, which they are used to utilising. this will stop now, also because in the new regulation there will be a cut-off limit facility which, on 1 july 2010, will become a default limit to apply automatically in order to protect our consumers. so it is a great day for europe and a great day for european consumers. thank you to parliament, which has acted very swiftly. i think the people outside will understand that this parliament works for the citizen.

union for the provision of pre-accession assistance for the period 2007-2013, which replaced the previous programmes for candidate and potential candidate countries. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the new mechanism comprises five components covering priorities defined according to the needs of the beneficiary countries, namely assistance covering transition we call for a strengthening of horizontal and regional programmes. we also call for a special emphasis on tackling corruption, organised crime and unemployment, especially youth unemployment. we call reconciliation and good neighbourly relations between the beneficiary countries, and between them and the member states of the union. in conclusion, we consider the preaccession assistance mechanism to be a rational and flexible instrument for optimising the provision of budgetary assistance to the candidate and potential candidate countries. but to be most effective, it needs to be continually aligned with the accession priorities and the social and political specificities of each country. to this end, parliament is committed to playing a substantial role in the implementation and adaptation of this instrument.
madam president, there is no doubt that in europe we are today paying the consequences of an anarchic, eccentric american, anglo-saxon model of financial market organization, which has learned to operate without rules, without supervision, without democratic accountability and, of course, has polluted the world and european economy. the texts we are discussing today and voting on tomorrow are building a shield of protection for the citizen here in europe. a shield that will protect him against this paradox we are living in today: that the money flows are transnational and the rules of supervision and accountability, where they exist, are national. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union. this, of course, leaves two big questions to be answered. the first question is: why did we have to live through the crisis to react? why did we have to wait for all this to happen before we had rules? the answer will come from the people, by rewarding those who demanded legislation, and punishing those who wanted to convince us that self-regulation is the solution to all the ills we live with today. the second question is whether these texts which we are discussing today will be the only ones or whether there will be a total overhaul, a total revision of the legislative and regulatory framework. the answer to this second question will be given by us because, as co-legislators, we will be pressing for it not to be confined to the gauzès report on the rating agencies which did not see the iceberg approaching the titanic and thus created what was created, but saw very quickly that some member states had to be downgraded because they did not have 'apparently' sufficient rating capacity. we must look at all this and structure it from the beginning: nothing will remain the same in the european union after the current crisis.

the next item is the joint debate on - report by mrs pervenche berès, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, on the package of medium-term financial assistance to member states' balances of payments (com (2009) 0169 - c6-0268/2009) and - statements by the council and the commission on the package of medium-term financial assistance to member states' balances of payments (com (2009) 0169 - c6-0268/2009)
member of the commission. − i am taking this debate on behalf of my colleague, mr almunia. the proposal on the table today concerns one of the core values of the union, namely solidarity. against the backdrop of the international financial crisis, the commission proposes further reinforced solidarity with the member states that do not yet benefit from the protective umbrella of the euro. the facility for supporting member states’ balances of payments is, by definition, foreseen for exceptional circumstances and was only used on a few occasions in the past. we do, however, live in exceptional times. as you know, the commission proposed last year an increase in the ceiling of outstanding loans that the community can provide to support member states’ balances of payments. the ceiling was raised from eur 12 billion to eur 25 billion, but a substantial part of this amount has already been earmarked. at the request of the countries concerned, the ecofin council decided to provide balance of payments support to hungary of up to eur 6.5 billion in november. on 20 january it decided to grant up to eur 3.1 billion to latvia, and the decision to provide up to eur 5 billion to romania is in the pipeline. already earmarked financial assistance under the regulation thus adds up to around eur 15 billion. there is, moreover, no immediate end in sight to either the financial crisis or global deleveraging, and ongoing financial stress in other member states could result in further needs for financial assistance. the european council of 19 and 20 march therefore welcomed the announcement by president barroso that the commission intended to make a proposal for increasing the ceiling of the community’s balance of payments support to eur 50 billion. the doubling of the ceiling will provide an important signal to financial markets of the european union’s strong commitment to helping member states under financial stress. the extension of the total possible credit line to eur 50 billion will provide a large buffer to cater for further possible needs for financial support. such strong signs of solidarity among member states should also help to calm financial investors’ fear of further financial market deterioration in the member states outside the euro area. by reducing the incentive for capital withdrawal, it would decrease the probability of balance of payments problems in the concerned countries. in this context i would like to express my gratitude and respect for the excellent spirit of cooperation at the european parliament and, in particular, the committee on economic and monetary affairs. the commission adopted this proposal just before the easter holidays on 8 april and only two weeks later you are now about to vote your legislative resolution and motion for resolution in plenary. thanks to your swift and efficient work, the ecofin council will be able to adopt the amended regulation on 5 may. the european union will thus be well equipped to react rapidly should the need for further balance of payments support arise. this is a powerful signal to member states that the european union is willing and ready to help and should be turned to in the first instance in case of balance of payments problems. this obviously does not exclude that a member state would also ask for assistance from other international organisations such as the imf, with which the commission has been closely cooperating on the latest financial support packages. i would like to conclude by saying that i agree with the statement in your draft motion for a resolution that the current international situation proves the relevance of the euro and that all member states outside the euro area should be encouraged to fulfil the maastricht criteria in order to join it.
the council stated that it would not make a statement on this. the debate is therefore continuing with speakers from the political groups.
in writing. the commission proposal drawing up the report creates a risk alert and information network between eu member states on their public and private infrastructure classified as 'vital'. this network is a first step towards giving private individuals, i.e. monopoly companies, whose facilities will be designated as critical infrastructure, powers in security matters which are currently the sole responsibility of the state. it opens the way for the labelling of 'terrorist acts' of mobilisations of the workers' and mass movement affecting any 'vital' infrastructure, including private premises (such as strikes in critical sectors - energy, telecommunications, etc. - or symbolic occupation of factories, businesses, etc., guarding strikes, mobilisations, etc.). it undermines the defence and sovereignty of the member states, abolishes the separation of their internal and external security and recognises the eu's direct role and involvement in it. the fight against the 'terrorist threat' is once again emerging as the eu's necessary pretext for completing its reactionary institutional framework which is essentially directed against the workers' and people's movement and shields the power of capital, further undermining the sovereign rights of the member states.
in writing. the eu is promoting the acceleration of capital restructuring in the mobile communications sector by extending the regulation to cover all services offered by mobile operators. the directive strengthens the position of european monopolies vis-à-vis their international competitors. it extends the regulation to facilitate takeovers and mergers that will provide higher returns on capital. the implementation of the directive has strengthened the monopolies. the alleged roaming pricing cap is circumvented by using minimum time pricing methods, increasing the price charged for the services offered and the profits of the companies, as acknowledged by the competent supervisory authorities. the planned capping of wholesale prices for other mobile services such as sms and voice mail will not benefit consumers but will further increase the profitability of capital. we are radically opposed to the liberalization and privatization of telecommunications markets, which leads to a deterioration in the quality of the services offered and the selling off of the people's property. we call on workers to express their universal opposition to the eu and its anti-people policy. to change the balance of power at the political level, and to express this in the next elections.
in writing. the report on the common migration policy welcomes the eu's pro-monopoly migration policy and the european parliament's contribution to it. the commission is also proposing to take measures for the next period against migrants in an even more reactionary direction. the report adopts the basic principle of eu immigration policy as a tool to serve the needs of the labour market, namely the profitability of european monopolies. to this end, it favours a policy of 'carrot and stick' towards immigrants. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. on the other hand, it welcomes the attraction and legalization of 'highly skilled' migrants, the circular and temporary migration needed by capital, and proposes measures to integrate legal migrants so that they are readily available to their employers. the only answer to this reactionary policy can be the integration of immigrants into the working class movement, the common struggle with local workers against the anti-people and anti-immigration policy of the eu and capital, the demand for their rights and the wealth they produce.
on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, the decision that we are about to take is a very important one. the socialist group is in favour of postponement for four reasons of principle. the first is the credibility and the role of our own parliament. it is very important to say, from the outset, what parliament can and cannot do. what it can and must do is control democratically the accounts of the council, too, notwithstanding the gentlemen’s agreement that has been put in place. the second reason is interinstitutional equilibrium. it is important that we do not do something that we cannot do as a parliament, but it is also very important to say that it is our right to have a view, to have an opinion on the council’s accounts, when these accounts are operational and where these operational accounts must be accounted for. this is the democratic principle and this is what we are about to do. so we are not saying we want to do something else. we are saying we want to do our duty. the third reason is the respect and the preparation for the lisbon treaty. you know very well, colleagues, that, with the lisbon treaty, the common external policy acquires much more depth and much more momentum. it is a much more important common policy and we cannot from the outset say that parliament will not have a bearing on that policy at all. we must have the opportunity now to say what the role of parliament is. the last – and probably the most important – point is transparency vis-à-vis the citizens. our role as a parliament is accountability vis-à-vis the citizens. we cannot, and we must not, say to the citizens that the big common policies, like the external and defence policy, will lie outside the democratic control of our parliament. so for those four important reasons of principle, we believe that we must vote in favour of postponement.

freedom of movement, because this is a matter of freedom first and foremost within our european union. it is not easy to set good standards, of course, but we are making a significant step forward today. i also appreciated the words of the council presidency, there are certainly still some reservations about the texts which the commission and parliament agree to adopt, but i also noted a general political openness on the part of the council, and this gives me good hope for the future. i am convinced that we will find, as we have done with the road transport question, a good compromise which will guarantee european citizens the possibility of moving freely and with adequate protection within the european union. i therefore believe that we are discussing issues that affect the lives of every citizen today, and that is why i am convinced that with regard to the bus proposal we should - the meeting of the tran committee on 30 march has already given me some encouraging elements - support the commission's approach to a wider scope of application covering all regular passenger transport services, international, national, regional, urban and suburban. this is in line with the objective of legislative simplification but also with the need to guarantee the same rights to all passengers without exclusion of principles. however, mr albertini, in view of the specific nature of the sector, the proposal leaves the member states free to exclude urban, suburban and regional transport services covered by public service contracts from the scope of the regulation, provided that such contracts offer passengers a high level of protection comparable to that provided for in the regulation. i think that is a reasonable solution which offers the required level of flexibility while safeguarding the basic rights of passengers. the liability of bus companies to passengers in the event of death or injury is another essential element of the proposal. there are too many differences in this respect in the community and this creates serious uncertainties for passengers. i would like to make a few points in this connection. first, this chapter does not deal with the question of insurance but only with the liability of carriers towards passengers. secondly, the bus company is not considered to be solely liable for damages and its right to seek compensation from third parties is not called into question. third, this chapter does not affect the amounts or the procedures laid down in the 2005 directive on insurance against civil liability in respect of the use of motor vehicles; and fourthly, the commission has not created a new model of accountability at all. what is proposed today is a liability regime which, taking account of the specificities of the sector, will be inspired by other modes of transport. as far as shipping is concerned, i believe that only one legislative instrument is necessary, even though there are differences of opinion both within parliament and in the council. let me emphasize one key point about the scope of the directive: compared to road, rail and air passengers, passengers travelling by sea and inland waterways are far fewer in number. of these, those using inland waterways represent only a small minority. it seems to me neither logical nor realistic to have separate regulations for sea passengers and those using inland waterways, especially since they often use the same type of vessel. i believe that the solution must be found in a single legislative instrument. on 30 march, a public debate of ministers in the transport council of the european union took place alongside the tran committee's discussion of the teychenné report. i am pleased to have had the opportunity to note that a certain degree of agreement between the commission, the member states and the european parliament on the objective of making an ambitious new regulation available to european passengers quickly has been a feature of this debate. in this context, the commission is naturally prepared to make all the necessary clarifications and improvements to the text to take better account of the specific nature of each type of service and thus provide the industry with the tools to apply the regulation with the necessary flexibility. i would like to stress once again the need to provide the means for the effective application of the future regulation. to this end, it is important that the national enforcement authorities report periodically on their activities, and that each member state may organise its national system as it prefers, for example by choosing whether to have one or more competent authorities.
the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - the mr president, the proposal for a regulation, as amended by the creative contribution of the european parliament, is a satisfactory boost to the rights of passengers in maritime transport. i think, however, that in a future legislative initiative, a horizontal approach - a single legal instrument covering all means of transport - would be more appropriate given the need for combined transport. it would be systematically preferable in the future to table separate proposals on passenger rights on the one hand and on persons with reduced mobility on the other, because the individual regulations have a different objective and different recipients. finally, i would like to point out the repeated monotonous approach of the commission (this does not concern vice-president tajani) to issues requiring wider and more multifaceted study, such as maritime transport. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted by the introduction of the new fishing fleet.
