refuse to see the reality of everyday life, that 15 of the 27 member states are using opt-outs, that at present there are simply not enough workers in many professions to cover these matters, especially in the new member states, then opt-outs must simply be a reality of everyday life by now. let us return to this question in 10 years' time, when the situation may be different in the member states. let us create the conditions so that member states do not have to opt-out, and then we may wonder if we will reach a compromise soon.
member of the commission. - ladies and gentlemen, arguments have been raised in the debate which have been used many times for good reasons. i think it's more than natural, because it was a debate that lasted five years, that affected us, and i want to thank everyone who took part in it very seriously. but the fact is, the result that we have is not a good result, it's not a stable result, and we're probably going to have to respond to it. i would also like to note that it is characteristic of this debate that it dealt with practically nothing other than opt-outs and perhaps a little bit of standby time. it did not take into account, for example, additional rest, changes in night work, etc. because really all of these changes, which were much more than just a question of on-call time or opting-out, all of these changes became, to some extent, hostages to two fundamental questions. in 1993, a directive was adopted which accepted the exception. this directive provided for certain types of revision, but not only of the opt-out but of the directive as such. in 2003, the european court of justice ruled that standby time spent at the workplace is working time. it's a decision that has a logic to it, and it's completely understandable to me. from that point on, the number of states using the opt-out began to increase rapidly. the reason is simple. in most countries, working time spent at work was not practically applied as actual working time and, once it had to be counted as fixed working time, countries began to opt-out in order to remain within the directive. ladies and gentlemen, as i have already indicated, the situation is complicated, it has its internal dynamics. in those five years of very deep debate, virtually every possible path forward was explored. i think, however, that our debate is not closed, that we need to look for other solutions, because the situation that exists now is simply not satisfactory. and it is not satisfactory for a number of reasons, not only because of standby time, which i personally consider to be the most serious, not only because of the opt-out, which is undoubtedly a substantial issue, but also because of a number of other reasons, such as night work, additional time off and a whole range of other things that can and, in my view, must progressively improve occupational safety and health, because this directive is a directive on occupational safety and health. and the organization of working time, as expressed in this directive, has this point of view, not just the universal one. ladies and gentlemen, it's just that the two co-legislators have not reached an agreement after five years of serious and lengthy debate, so we're at a certain point and we need to look for another way out. i'm sure the commission is ready.
the debate is closed. the court of justice shall have jurisdiction to hear and determine the appeal.

vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to reassure those members who have expressed some doubts about the text we are discussing. it is not road safety at stake at all, and i repeat, it is my priority to fight to try to reduce the number of accidents on the roads. i don't think we should confuse working hours with driving time. i fully understand the remarks made: the self-employed may work earlier, work later, be tired when they drive, but i don't think you can control a self-employed person in any job. of course the self-employed person is also aware of what he is doing, he can also load a truck and wait and rest for two hours, three hours, four hours, then he can also drive again in optimal conditions with regard to safety. it is very difficult to control any selfemployed person, any craftsman, any small businessman, who are the men and women who form the backbone of the economy of our european union. that said, we are concerned with ensuring the employment and safety of employees and of those workers who are apparently independent but are in fact employees. that is why the commission - and i think the rapporteur agrees with the commission - wants the work of 'so-called selfemployed' or 'so-called independent' people to be included under the umbrella of legislative control. i think that is an important signal, that this regulation is a regulation that responds to real needs, and i think it is right to stress once again how important it is to take another step forward. that is why i ask the socialist and green group to reflect on the remarks that have been made and to understand that the commission has made road safety a priority and will continue to do so, but this legislation does not affect road safety, but only aims to regulate better the working time of workers, especially those who work in the road transport sector, and to regulate time, to equate 'so-called selfemployed' with 'real employees' because in reality they are not selfemployed but workers who are in fact employees. that is why i wanted to reassure once again all those who had expressed doubts, because i believe that the text that can be adopted is a good text that goes in the general direction of the interests of the european citizens.

i have to say that i am not in favour of this. - madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by welcoming the european commission's proposal to recast the energy labelling directive with the main aim of extending its scope to all energy-consuming appliances for domestic, commercial and industrial use, as well as to products linked to energy consumption. the directive is part of a wider package of legislative proposals aimed at promoting energy efficiency, a top priority for the union as it can play a key role in strengthening energy security, reducing carbon emissions and revitalising the european economy to emerge faster from recession and crisis. i would like to thank all my colleagues for their proposals, and in particular the shadow rapporteurs, the pse secretariat and the itre secretariat, because with their help we have succeeded - i believe - in strengthening the energy labelling directive. that is why we attach particular importance to the provisions relating to public procurement, we attach particular importance to the introduction of incentives to promote the most efficient appliances, and that is why we want to strengthen the provisions relating to market control and surveillance. i shall only dwell on two subjects which have been hotly debated. the first concerns our proposal to include energy consumption in advertising, which has provoked a disproportionate reaction from the european publishers' association and from the owners of radio and television stations. i say disproportionate, because there have been extreme arguments such as that the freedom of the press is being curtailed, that advertising revenue is being reduced for the sensitive media sector and - finally - that funding for a free and independent press is being undermined. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we believe that advertising of products relating to energy consumption should refer to either energy consumption, energy saving or energy labelling only when the advertising includes references to technical specifications or information. where advertising does not include such references, there is no obligation to refer to energy consumption. i have left the question of the layout of energy labels, which has emerged as the most pressing issue in this directive, to the last. this directive will not have achieved its objective, that is to say, it will be ineffective, if energy labelling is not easily recognisable and comparable by the consumers to whom it is primarily addressed in order to help them make the best possible choices. we now have a successful energy labelling model, the a to g scale, where a is the most efficient product and g is the least efficient. a scale currently used as a standard in many countries outside the european union. it is certainly very successful, it is very recognisable and it has contributed a great deal to energy savings. of course, he's got enough problems. the most serious of these problems, the main reason for these problems, is that the marking is not accompanied by an indication of its period of validity. when there is no indication of the duration of the label, when a product is classified as category a and remains in that category for its lifetime - at a time when much more energy-efficient products are coming onto the market - it is obvious that we are sending confusing messages. so how do we deal with this? by requiring the label to have a specific lifetime, for example 3-5 years, and after this period a review of the energy efficiency scale based on progress in the product category concerned. in conclusion, i would like to say, madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, that i am deeply convinced that in the course of the discussions that will follow, in order to be able to reach an agreement at second reading, we will find the best possible solution. a solution that will be recognised by consumers and will promote innovation in european industry.

in writing. i am voting against amendment no 54 because it is an important political message to the commission and the council. the european parliament supports the rights of drivers and rejects any competition between salaried and self-employed drivers. the proposal would exclude selfemployed drivers from the scope of the current directive and, for the first time in european legislation, would attempt to distinguish between 'genuinely selfemployed' and 'pseudo-selfemployed', which is however incomplete and may open the floodgates to the interpretation of other provisions of community law. it is a victory for road safety and social europe.
in writing. the rejection of the commission's proposal for a directive is a manoeuvre by the forces of the 'euro-path' in the run-up to the european elections. the kke opposed the commission's proposal from the outset, voting against it in the committee responsible and in the plenary. inform the workers, support their mobilizations. the exemption for the selfemployed serves only the interests of the monopoly transport companies, it harms the interests of workers, of the selfemployed, and it creates enormous risks for road safety. it will further worsen the working conditions of drivers, increase the hours of work-driving to as much as 84 hours a week and intensify the exploitation of working drivers to the limit. the mobilization of workers and the fear of condemnation in the european elections forced a significant part of the meps of the parties of the euro-road to vote in favour of rejecting the proposal. however, workers should be aware that the monopoly groups will come back to impose their demands finding the parties of capital willing to satisfy them. this conquest shows the strength and importance of the workers' struggle. we stress, however, that it may prove to be temporary if the workers' and people's movement does not organize its counter-attack, does not set the conditions for radical changes in the level of power and the economy.
in writing. the proposal for a directive on the protection of animals used for scientific purposes is aimed more at completing the internal market, competition and cost containment in the field of research than at protecting animals. the multinationals are demanding ever greater profits from research. the animals used in it are usually slaughtered and tortured for the sake of increasing profits rather than serving scientific needs. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the animal welfare provisions are respected. research requires experiments to address important public health problems and many still incurable diseases. animals are often essential to these investigations. the protection of animals, however, like the protection of public health, requires a fight against monopolies, against the power of capital which patents medicines in order to secure huge profits from the commercialization of health. a struggle to free research from the shackles of capital and to use the scientific findings of research to meet the needs of the people.

president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, as some of you have already said, this is the last time that they will be here in plenary, in a debate before a european council. i shall therefore not be able to see them in july or september, as the mandate of this commission expires, as you know, at the end of october. i would therefore like to say, once again, to all those colleagues who have served the european project, who are members of the european parliament: 'you can be proud of the work done by the european parliament'. if we take a look back at the work of this parliament, i think we can all be proud of it. the truth is that europe has been at the forefront of many things, whether it is the fight against climate change, the fight for a new energy security policy and everything that is now necessary to respond to the financial and economic crisis which, of course, is affecting europe very seriously. but i didn't hear enough today. this crisis is affecting the united states of america. that's where it started. it affects russia, it affects japan, it affects china, even. it affects emerging markets. and europe, from the very beginning, wanted to respond to this crisis, not just immediately, but also through programmes on the big issues of regulation and supervision. so that's the message i'd like to give you right now. some of you have challenged me in reference to the election period we are living through today. but i don't think i can and i shouldn't raise them now. the future commission must, of course, define its programme. but it is not for me to say what the manifesto of the next commission will be. so i take your challenge. i see it as a sign of trust. but i can't answer that right now. i believe that we have every reason to be proud of the outcome of this parliament's mandate. but i prefer to concentrate now on the european council in june, which has very important issues to deal with and which requires great responsibility. firstly, the question of the lisbon treaty and the transition we have to make from one mandate of the european parliament to another, but also the constitution of a new commission. it is an extremely delicate challenge - as some of you have said - since we do not yet have the lisbon treaty, this treaty which has been signed by the 27 governments but not yet ratified by all of them. it is not the fault of the european parliament. nor is it the commission's. the truth is that governments have signed a treaty that they have not been able to get final approval for. and because of this delay, we are faced with a serious problem of institutional transition. this is a problem which requires a great sense of responsibility on the part of all: council, parliament and commission. i therefore welcome the very wise words of the czech presidency and of the members who have spoken on this subject. we will have to find solutions in full respect of the existing treaty we are a community of law; we cannot suspend a treaty that is in force. in full respect of the law, we must find sensible solutions that guarantee the stability of the european project. and that is what i shall be calling on the heads of state and government to do at the european council in june. the other question we have to solve is the answer to the economic question. i think we must also recognize that in this case it is not responsible to present new programmes every day. this is a mistake. i believe that leadership is primarily an exercise of responsibility. it's not about, say, entertaining the gallery. it would be easy for the commission to come up with new ideas every day, knowing full well that they will have no chance of being put into practice. but we will not do so because we believe that our task as the european commission representing the general european interest is not just to play the game of ease, to make announcements, but really to propose measures and lines that have a chance of bringing europe as a whole together, the other institutions, the parliament and the council, and bringing together the european citizens. the commission has indeed made the first proposals for a european recovery plan, with the instruments we have at our disposal, no doubt. you know that most of these instruments are national, and in particular the budgets: they are national budgets. the european budget is tiny compared to national budgets. and some of those who are impatient, who are demanding much more from us, may be able to help the commission in the next financial perspective to convince the member states, especially where the parties voted for a maximum of 1% of the resources - the 1% club which has limited the financial resources. this would be an interesting thing to achieve in the next parliament. because we will act, i can tell you, as early as 2009; we will now present the lines of the financial review and how we will proceed in the future. but do not ask the european institutions for anything they cannot give you now. ask us what we can do, because - and i speak with great sincerity - particularly about certain criticisms which, i know, come from people who share the same european ideal as i do - i think it is a fundamental mistake to always criticise europe for what it has not yet done, when you know very well that if it has not yet done it is not the fault of the community institutions, it is mainly the fault of a lack of ambition at national level. it is not fair to criticise in this way, and it does not make it any easier for us to carry forward our european project. the truth is that the commission has made ambitious proposals, that we are keeping the economic situation under constant review, that we will make further proposals if necessary. but now we believe that the important thing is to focus on the execution - i said execution, implementation, not gestures - of what we have decided, because we also have very serious concerns about sustainability, because public debt in europe is reaching a really worrying level. the situation in some of our member states is very serious. we must therefore make proposals that can address these concerns. i also think we should highlight what europe is doing. i understand that, for matters of immediate policy, particularly in the context of the european elections, the temptation is great - especially for those who are members of an opposition party in their own country - to sometimes oppose europe. i ask you to be careful, because tomorrow you may be in power, and tomorrow you may ask people to vote for europe, and people will not vote for a europe which you have described as a wild europe. the people will vote for a europe which has the support of all the political forces of the right, the left and the centre which are reflected in the european project. that, in my view, is a great challenge. i am for a political europe but i am against political politics when we talk about the european project. and i'm against the abusive partitioning in partisan terms. the truth is that we can only make europe with the great political families. the commission is made up of these families: the epp, the socialists, the social democrats, the liberals and other independent groups. the commission will continue to present itself in this way. i understand that from an immediate political point of view, particularly in the european parliament, particularly in the context of the european parliament elections, we want, each of you wants to highlight your own programme, your own party. i'm a party man. i was elected to the portuguese parliament at the age of 29. i was leader of the opposition and i was prime minister. so i'm a party man. but be careful! european politics needs people who are partisan but who are able to go beyond their own party, who are able to form coalitions above parties. if we are supranational, we must also have a vision which sometimes goes beyond that of our own party. i would like to warn you against this, with all due respect, because i am well aware that most of you are now involved in a sometimes very demanding political election campaign, in which there are also national issues at stake. but i think it's important for the future. if we want to understand the great challenges facing europe, we must unite all europeans, from the left, from the right, from the centre, all those who share the essence of the european project, and not succumb to this push for artificial partisan dramatization, because in fact, most of the time, it is artificial. i can tell you that in the commission the proposals on hedge funds - which some of you have criticised - were the subject of a consensus in the commission between all the commissioners, members of the socialist family, members of the liberal family and members of the epp. there was no split on this issue. i understand that in the political struggle it is interesting to focus criticism on one or another commissioner, but that is not politically or even intellectually fair. in closing, i'd like to say that i'm growing more and more in awe of the founding fathers. the truth - let's be clear - is that the czech republic is facing a political problem. the deputy prime minister, mr vondra, has clearly admitted this, with great honesty. it is obviously difficult for a country holding the presidency of the union to have to face an internal political crisis and replace its own government. the truth is that despite this, we are achieving great results, thanks to you, the european parliament. i would like to say that it is also thanks to us, the commission, that we have made proposals. but it is also thanks to the czech presidency. we are finalising 50 codecision cases, some of them extremely difficult. we are able to do this at a time when the country holding the presidency is in the midst of a political crisis. i think that we should pay tribute to the institutional capacity of europe, which means that even in a situation like this the european council is able to deliver results. that is why, when i paid tribute to the czech presidency, and in particular to deputy prime minister vondra, i did so sincerely, because i know how extremely difficult it is - i have seen it every day - to work in these conditions and still achieve results. so here we have a choice to make. the choice we have to make is to highlight, while acknowledging, for those who are the most ambitious - of which i am one - that we have not yet reached the goals we set for ourselves. we must also highlight what we have done and what we have achieved together. others, on the other hand, focus on what has not yet been possible. the message they send to the european citizens is always negative. i've said it many times. the pessimism of pro-europeans is sometimes more serious than the euroscepticism of anti-europeans because it does not carry a message of hope for those who believe in europe. i would like to thank the vice-premier, mr vondra, for his speeches, the czech presidency and all of you for what we have achieved together for our europe, which is a great project of peace, freedom and solidarity, despite all the differences of opinion.

the next item is the joint debate on - the statements by the council and the commission on the preparation of the employment summit, - the report by gabriele stauner, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the european globalisation adjustment fund (com (2008) 0867 - c6-0518/2008 - 2008/0267 (cod)) (a6-0242/2009), - the report by josé albino silva peneda, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the renewed social agenda (com (2008) 2330 (ini)) (a6-0241/2009), and the report by jean lambert, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the active integration of people excluded from the labour market (a35/06/2009) (a23/06 (ini)).
the committee on women's rights and gender equality, which drew up the report, is also proposing that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the situation of women in the european union. - the mr president, on behalf of the committee on women's rights, we have delivered an opinion on the report by mrs lambert on the active inclusion of people excluded from the labour market, and i congratulate her on her willingness to incorporate as fully as possible the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. gender equality and, in general, respect for the principle of non-discrimination are essential prerequisites for active integration into the labour market and the social integration which must accompany it. in particular, i think it is important to emphasize the support of family members of all ages, solidarity between the generations and the help to be given to vulnerable groups of the population in the difficult situations that a family may be in, so that it can - at any time - be useful to society without the difficult circumstances leaving their mark on its life. the transition from one situation to another is therefore very important and must be supported by the means available from the state, the social partners, the social partners and the voluntary sector, so that society feels solidarity and mutual responsibility for all its members. i hope that mrs lambert's report will give a boost to the resolution - as has been corrected by my political group - to include not only income support, but to include an overall support for decent living conditions covering both small and large participants and non-participants in the labour market.
member of the commission. − the commission is a full participant in the g20 and is participating fully in this process. i have already mentioned some of the measures the commission would like to encourage member states to take. it is fully involved in the g20 process.
member of the commission. − well, there is a treaty and the treaty is very clear. we know that there will be a new irish referendum perhaps on the lisbon treaty, but these articles are very clear on how the competences are divided in text, and the commission has no intention of writing a new treaty with different responsibilities. this is also partly the answer on using tax policy for creating jobs. as different countries are in different situations, a europe-wide taxation instrument will not perhaps achieve the same goals as in a particular member state. taxation instruments are sometimes good to really create incentives or develop the economy, but this is very much dependent on the situation in particular member staes.
member of the commission. - madam president, the commission is fully committed to helping to protect people from earthquakes. such protection should be part of an integrated approach to address situations that may arise from various risk causes. the commission has recently adopted a communication entitled 'the community approach to the prevention of natural and man-made disasters', which covers, inter alia, earthquakes. the communication follows on from the actions undertaken by the commission itself in recent years, which are in line with many of the recommendations made by the european parliament in its 2007 resolution. this action shall in particular relate to the dissemination of best practices, the development of common approaches to risk assessment, mapping, training and awareness-raising, while seeking to improve the coherence and synergy of the existing financial and legislative prevention instruments, thereby enhancing the added value of european union action. the commission will also make every effort to mitigate the effects of earthquakes by encouraging member states to fully incorporate the common european design codes for buildings and public works, in particular european code 8, into their respective national regulations. the commission is looking forward with great interest to the european parliament's response to the approach proposed in its communication.
madam president, i'm sorry, but the issue remains. i am not sure that the commission is prepared to do what it did for the floods. what's the difference? i am particularly struck by the fact that this debate is taking place in the wake of the recent disaster in italy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. do you honestly think that the reference to the earthquake in the communication in three points is sufficient to compensate for the great need we have in europe for a communication specifically on earthquakes? honestly, i'd like an answer.
member of the commission. - madam president, the communication we made in february 2009 addresses the whole issue in an integrated way, regardless of the source of the risks. we believe this approach is the best. as for the floods, we have a report of water shortages and drought. there are communications about specific risks or specific problems that exist, but we feel that the integrated approach we want to use is the most appropriate. we are waiting for parliament's response and if there are important reasons why we should abandon this approach, we will consider it. i should like to point out, however, that the commission's communication will do little to prevent the consequences of earthquakes. the member states - who are zealously upholding the principle of subsidiarity in their competence in this area - should therefore be asked to incorporate into their national law the european union's provisions on buildings, either the eurocode 8 for new buildings or the provisions on the armour of buildings for old ones. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the commission is not prepared to take any further action to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.
madam president, i too am impressed by the commission's position expressed here today, because, of course, the answer on the question of the principle of subsidiarity is respected and i fully agree with the specific suggestions, but this does not solve the problem of a collective policy proposal from the european commission which includes all aspects of disasters caused by earthquakes. mr pirker added one more and we would certainly find more. the question therefore remains: why, in addition to the principle of subsidiarity and the specific measures mentioned by the commissioner, do we not have a comprehensive proposal from the european commission?
member of the commission. - madam president, as i said earlier, we are awaiting the european parliament's response to our communication on earthquake prevention. when we have the answer we will consider whether this approach or the other approach is better. in particular, there are now specific provisions for nuclear installations and the seveso 2 directive for industrial installations.
member of the commission. − solar-thermal electricity was never excluded from the european economic recovery plan. it was not included for the reason that we addressed the most urgent energy needs. that means that we looked at the issues where the crisis has had the greatest impact and where projects could not materialise. the first question was interconnection. any power production, especially from new energy sources, cannot function if there are grid issues. so we addressed as a matter of urgency the electricity grids and interconnection between member countries. on top of that we have had the gas crisis, which clearly showed that in some cases missing physical infrastructure has a huge impact as regards gas supply disruption. this is the easiest way to respond to the security of gas supplies. and we addressed two other issues which i think would definitely be postponed if they did not have support from the community: firstly, offshore wind energy, which is rather a large project that needs to be connected to the mainland grid, and, secondly, carbon capture and storage, where we really need to encourage industry to come with a full-scale project as soon as possible. for this reason we have not considered other eligible areas. that is why it was not included, not because i do not believe in it. i believe in solar thermal energy but we addressed the most urgent needs. what sources are available to support the further evolution of solar thermal electricity? first there is the seventh framework programme. we have eur 2.35 billion for the years 2007-2013. second, it is one area where you can provide the tool. from next year we have an opportunity to provide 300 million emission allowances for new entrants. renewable energy projects, such as solar thermal electricity, are eligible. so if the projects are there they are very welcome. we can also use funds from the european regional development fund. we encourage more use of synergy energy sources; in particular we increased the limits of how much renewable energy could be used in relation to housing and energy efficiency. so overall eligibility for these measures has been increased. on top of this, in the summer or early autumn, the commission will table a new communication on financing low-carbon technologies. we will try to elaborate on it so that these technologies get support, not only from the community budget but also from industry and the member states, so that this support is targeted and so that we get the new technological development very quickly to achieve our goal of 20% renewable energy by 2020, but perhaps we can be even more ambitious in this respect. so we have not excluded it. it is just that we have a lot of areas that we need to address and we have taken the most urgent areas where money needs to be invested now.
member of the commission. − in establishing the list we looked at the majority of the projects and we also involved the member states, so at this stage i have no reason to believe that we will be unable to disburse or commit all the money. if there should be some delays, or if some projects are not sufficiently prepared, we will have a report in march 2010. so, if there is a possibility that part of the money is available, i promised at trialogues that the commission will consider, if appropriate, making a necessary proposal. but it is premature to promise it because it depends how well we can commit the money, as today there are a limited number of projects where we believe that they have advanced enough to use this money.
member of the commission. − well, my colleague stavros dimas has already replied to a supplementary question on this issue. basically, nuclear energy is established in a way that means that each country has a nuclear safety regulator who has full responsibility for implementing nuclear safety measures. at the same time, each member state decides whether or not to use nuclear energy. this is subject to the euratom treaty, and very clearly there are additional requirements. the additional requirements are that each project that is submitted is also analysed by the commission and we make a recommendation on the projects to be developed. we did that in the case of belene and also of mochovce. there is also a clear obligation that the requirements of the environmental impact assessment should be fulfilled, as laid down in community law, and the additional requirements of the un/ece espoo convention on environmental impact assessment in a transboundary context will also be applicable. so whatever project is being proposed, we are not discriminating between member states. there are clear requirements to be fulfilled and each project is analysed on its own merits, based on the community acquis and on environmental legislation. i can assure you that this process is so watertight that no permission has ever been given to build a nuclear power installation unless all the safety requirements can be guaranteed.
madam president, i would like to ask, in connection with what the commissioner said about the evaluation of each proposal, whether a study can be made in advance of the region in question, based on euro-mediterranean cooperation and the specific nature of the region, so that there are criteria which each country should take into account in advance and which it should take into account in its planning.
member of the commission. − first of all, each and every country develops its own legislation that decides on the process of how the application should be made. later, the process very much relates to, and is based on, the national legislation and euratom legislation. as a result, we cannot take a regional approach. we need to look at the specific situation in each and every member state and, at the same time, each and every project. we cannot just consider an average situation in the region and say that this region is not suitable for using nuclear power. that would be untrue because you cannot develop a project that could be used in more or less every place. it is a question of the costs, conditions and time needed. so, we cannot go with a regional approach. we really need to look at each and every concrete project because only in this way can we guarantee full safety in this project.
member of the commission. − on the second part, i believe that, with this high-level group of nuclear regulators, we can move forward with stronger standards. this is just the point of departure; it was the lowest common denominator we could have developed. at the same time, because of the sensitivity of the issue – politically and also, sometimes, culturally – i strongly doubt that the european regulator could play the role required. i would not say the european regulator could not be trusted, but that regulator needs to keep a distance from nuclear power installations. i believe that full responsibility for safety should lie at all times with a national regulator, because that is the best way and that will always avoid the debate of whether the central regulator is pushing hard enough for nuclear energy etc. it is good to establish a common framework, but responsibility must remain with all the regulators who, as is proposed in the nuclear safety directive, have full powers and all the necessary instruments to implement this policy. we have very good nuclear safety regulators in the union, so an organisational shake-up is not necessary. we just need to increase the binding standards, starting from the level we have now, and working together, thus demonstrating to the world that standards can be increased in the nuclear industry.
member of the commission. − i am very glad that you put this question, because i am very proud of one of our achievements, and that is the covenant of mayors. in 2008 we supported the initiative from cities to have a covenant of mayors. this means that cities make a binding obligation to reduce co2 by more than 20% by 2020. they develop sustainable energy action plans for this. they have also founded a covenant of mayors’ office for coordination and promotion. we support these actions through the joint research centre, to provide scientific background and technical support for the preparation, implementation and evaluation of the sustainable energy action plans. for smaller municipalities, the commission negotiates with other national, regional and local administrations to ensure their support for the smaller cities. an advanced system of good practice, the benchmarks of excellence, is also intended to be implemented this year. we have also created a financial support facility through the european investment bank (eib), in which we place the grants, and also use the eib to target sustainable development in the cities. the eib has already been involved in specific projects, such as with barcelona, on which negotiations are quite advanced. i know that the city of cork has not yet signed this covenant, so it is still up to the city of cork to use this opportunity. it is a good way to really mobilise citizens behind these initiatives. at the same time there are also other instruments for sustainable development, and the cohesion policy provides significant support to cities to implement sustainable energy policies and actions. in total, for the period 2007-2013, eur 9 billion from cohesion policy funds will be directly invested in sustainable energy initiatives in the eu. the southern and eastern operational programme, which includes the city of cork, will invest eur 669 million in the region and contribute to reaching lisbon growth and jobs objectives. these cohesion funds will directly support sustainable energy projects in the region, and will help to mobilise further national investment. funding may be used to stimulate energy efficiency and renewable energy production, the development of efficient energy management systems and the promotion of clean and sustainable public transport, particularly in urban areas. the operational programme includes the ‘gateway challenge fund’, which is based on an integrated sustainable development strategy for cork. finally, the regional development fund was recently changed to give all member states the possibility to use regional funds for energy efficiency improvements and the integration of renewable energy into existing housing. this can be as much as 4% of the total regional fund allocation. so, to summarise, i believe that we have created a framework for cities that share an ambition to develop sustainable models for their citizens, and that is the covenant of mayors. the covenant of mayors includes sympathisers like new york and rochester, and has participants from other non-eu countries, so it offers a lot of knowledge through the office we operate. as concerns financial support, we do not at this stage have any programme dedicated to energy efficiency, but we have the structural funds, we have an intelligent energy europe programme and we have also the european investment bank. we are using all those instruments to support these initiatives. the european investment bank is very active in this process. however, at this stage it is not so much an issue of finance, but more about good projects and really dedicated policy from the cities. that is my answer to your very important question. i believe that cities have every opportunity to lead the way towards a sustainable energy future.
member of the commission. − we are trying to respond to this request. we are planning, by the end of this year, to develop a new energy efficiency action plan that will address this. we are also drawing up a communication on the financing of low-carbon energy sources. it is very important for stakeholders to take the initiative, and i expect cities to put forward proposals and put pressure on members of the european parliament and on member states to create europe-wide instruments in the areas concerned. without this bottom-up approach, we will fail to use the opportunities that exist. european cities are so diverse, and there are so many good ideas, that we should not just try to apply a top-down approach to each and every city. i believe that the covenant of mayors will provide much-needed intellectual input and experience to the policies we need to formulate to achieve sustainable cities. it is not only about savings. there are many issues involved. it is also about smart grids, the integration of renewable energy and sustainable transport – hydrogen buses, electric vehicles. there are plenty of opportunities. each and every city could make its own programme, because what the covenant of mayors has done is make an obligation to reduce co2 by more than 20%. that does not necessarily mean through energy efficiency. it could be a completely different policy area. it very much depends on local circumstances.
member of the commission. − since the outbreak of the economic crisis, the european union has taken a firm stance against straight protectionism, consistent with its commitment to fair and open trade. the commission is intensively monitoring protectionist measures put in place by its major trading partners with a view to appropriate action. furthermore, the eu has strongly supported the initiative of the world trade organisation to report on trade-related developments during the economic and financial crisis. since the beginning of the current crisis, more than ever the eu has been committed to multilateralism, to transparency and to open markets based on rules that benefit developed and developing countries alike. the eu has consistently argued that an ambitious and balanced doha round outcome is among the most important instruments in preventing trade protectionism and in boosting the economy. this is also the message put forward by the wto in the recent trade policy review of the eu trade policy, which praised the role played by the eu in strengthening the multilateral trading system. the key role the doha round could play in the current crisis was also recognised in the g20 global plan for recovery and reform issued on 2 april. the g20 leaders expressed their renewed commitment to reaching a swift conclusion of the doha round. finally, the eu’s commitment to open trade goes hand in hand with its commitment to sustainable development, which is an overarching policy objective for the eu, including in the context of various trade policy initiatives. sustainable development and, in particular, environmental and social considerations are important components in our trade policy. this is witnessed by the inclusion of sustainable development provisions focusing on labour and environmental standards in our bilateral trade agreements.
member of the commission. − reports by the wto and the commission’s own monitoring show that there is no imminent risk of a generalised protectionist escalation. none of the large trading nations has yet resorted to across-the-board restrictions on trade and investment. developments in certain countries – for instance, russia, indonesia and argentina – need close monitoring as these countries have until now been the greatest users of border measures to restrict trade. overall, the most targeted sectors are agriculture and food products, iron and steel, metal, automobiles, textiles and toys.
member of the commission. − the commission is doing everything possible to ensure that this monitoring is strong in every aspect, especially on the issue that you raised. i could convey the message to commissioner ashton in order to have a more specific view and a more specific answer. but so far there are no disturbing signals.
member of the commission. − the commission is aware of the issues raised in relation to canada’s intellectual property rights protection and enforcement. we are extremely concerned that canada’s proposed reform of its copyright act appears to be being given so little priority. reasonable protection of intellectual property rights is essential for the future survival of the creative industries. while several member states are now eagerly discussing how intellectual property rights can better be adapted to the online world, we must indeed still pay attention to the risks of internet piracy originating from third countries. one of the key issues in play is how to strike a fair balance between the interests of rights holders and those of consumers, while respecting data protection rules. you will remember that this was a key debating point of the commission creative content online initiative launched by the communication of 3 january 2008. the commission’s concerns have been put forcefully to the canadian authorities for quite some time now. the fact we will soon start negotiations for a free trade agreement with canada will give us a new opportunity to bring about significant improvement of ipr protection in canada. in the mean time, the commission will be taking the matter up with the canadian authorities at the highest level. it will also call for the revision of canadian copyright law to be initiated as soon as possible and preferably during the parliament’s present session.
member of the commission. − as i said before, a trade agreement will provide the legal framework with which to address some of the eu’s trade irritants and issues with canada, such as its ipr enforcement standards and certain anti-competitive practices. we are optimistic since we will have negotiations on an fta.
member of the commission. − the negotiations on a free trade agreement with korea are the most advanced of the eu’s ongoing trade negotiations. after two years of negotiations, we now have a comprehensive agreement within reach with the eu’s fourth-largest trading partner outside europe. the free trade agreement with korea would create new market access in many areas of interest to eu exporters. by way of illustration: the agreement would swiftly eliminate eur 1.6 billion of customs duties to the benefit of eu exporters; it would offer solid disciplines on non-tariff barriers in priority sectors such as the automotive, pharmaceutical, or consumer electrics sectors; it would provide significant new market access in services of special interest to eu service providers; it would offer the most ambitious package yet on intellectual property rights, government procurement, sustainable development and other rules issues; and it would foresee novel ways of involving civil society in the monitoring of how the free trade agreement is implemented, and protect our sensitivities with appropriate arrangements. the free trade agreement would also present a useful point of reference for other negotiations. moreover, concluding that agreement now would demonstrate the commitment of both sides to open markets as one response to the current economic environment. at the end of the eighth round of negotiations in march 2009, both sides made important technical progress at their level, but a certain number of difficult issues remained unresolved. eu trade commissioner ashton and korean trade minister kim subsequently met in london on 2 april 2009. despite intensive discussions, it was not possible to bridge gaps on the remaining issues, such as duty drawback and rules of origin. the commission is currently consulting on the best way forward.
member of the commission. − in contrast, the member states have recently confirmed their interest in concluding an ambitious and balanced agreement with korea. as usual, the commission will defend the principles on the basis of which we negotiate with all countries.
member of the commission. − i understand your concerns about the situation in the car industry. from the start of negotiations with korea, the commission has invested considerable resources in obtaining a good result for the eu car industry. european car exporters will benefit from a combination of the elimination of korean duties on cars and the effective removal of technical barriers. the most substantial barriers will be removed from day one of the agreement.
member of the commission. − the commission is currently still engaged in negotiations with the united states, and we are very optimistic that a mutually acceptable solution can emerge very soon. in the last few weeks we had intensive transatlantic contacts on this issue. the eu trade commissioner catherine ashton and us trade representative ron kirk agreed on 22 april to hold further talks in order to find a negotiated solution to the long-running dispute over hormone-treated beef. they confirmed their wish to resolve this difficult issue that affects businesses and consumers in both the eu and the us and, in order to facilitate the negotiations for a solution, commissioner ashton and us trade representative kirk agreed that the imposition of the so-called ‘carousel’ sanctions by the us on certain eu products would be postponed by a further two weeks beyond the original 23 april deadline. the commission is committed to doing its best to find a positive and lasting solution to this issue, and talks are currently being held by senior officials from both sides. the commission’s final objectives in these negotiations are to definitively prevent activation of the ‘carousel’ sanctions and to have the retaliatory measures currently in place revoked. we are confident that an agreement is within reach that would fully safeguard our public health and consumer protection objectives in this issue.
madam president, in the absence of the commissioner responsible, mrs ashton, i would like to thank commissioner kuneva for her reply and i would ask you, commissioner, to convey to mrs ashton my thanks for the mediation, for the consultations with the american side on the lifting of the 'karousel' countermeasures applied by the united states of america. this is an action which, if anything, goes beyond the partnership between the united states of america and the european union, two leading trade actors with a dominant role in the doha round negotiations under the auspices of the world trade organisation.
member of the commission. − mr papastamkos, i shall gladly convey your message to commissioner ashton and, without going too far in what i am saying, i think the results will be really quite satisfactory.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i think that it should be pointed out first of all that it is not for the commission to interpret the judgments of the court of justice. these judgments answer questions referred by national courts for a preliminary ruling in order to ensure the uniform application of community law. under article 16 of regulation (ec) no 261/2004, which concerns the rights of passengers in the event of denied boarding, cancellation of flights or long delays, member states are responsible for the application of that regulation and are required to prosecute air carriers for non-compliance with its provisions. in dealing with complaints from passengers which are forwarded to them, the competent national authorities, designated by the member state in accordance with the regulation, must obviously take account of the guidance provided by the court of justice. the commission's role is to monitor the proper implementation of the regulation by the competent authorities in the light, in particular, of the relevant judgments of the court of justice. the last meeting between the commission and the competent national authorities was held at the beginning of december last year, a few weeks before the court of justice's decision. the commission has scheduled another meeting on the 14th of this month to discuss the recent decision of the court of justice in case c-549/07. the national authorities will have the opportunity to comment on the judgment and to explain how they assess, in the light of the case law, the behaviour of airlines towards passengers in the event of cancellation and the practical consequences of the judgment.
vice-president of the commission. - thank you for speaking in italian. i know you're of italian descent; thank you again. of course the commission will monitor what is happening because i think passenger rights are very important to all of us. that is one of the aims of my work as commissioner. i said that when i got the consent of parliament. i will therefore continue to ensure that checks are carried out to verify that member states respect passenger rights. we are all committed, including commissioner kuneva, to trying to defend the rights of citizens everywhere effectively.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, the commission intends to report to parliament and the council on the operation and results of regulation (ec) no 261/2004 in a communication which is expected to be adopted in the second half of this year. the document will analyse four years of operation of the regulation and propose further possible actions to be developed, with the aim of reducing the number of incidents and improving the protection of air passengers' rights. all airports will be checked. i hope that the commission's work will improve the situation and give the citizens the answers they are looking for. i can give you my assurance of commitment as commissioner. i started it, i'm going to keep it, i'm confirming my commitment.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission shares the view expressed in your question that the commission's decisions have obviously led to the orderly sale of certain assets of olympic airlines and olympic airways services. they have certainly been successful in terms of competition and transport policy. the same model was used for alitalia and could be used for all airlines in serious difficulty. in short, this decision has created a new model of airline, the one that should be the airline of the future: no longer state-owned companies, but entirely private companies. this is the work which the commission has tried to do, and i believe that this objective of privatization is also the objective of other airlines, such as austrian airlines, malev and czech airlines. however, the treaty requires the commission to remain neutral as regards the public or private ownership of undertakings. the commission is in favour of consolidating the community's aviation sector, which it considers to be still too fragmented, especially in the face of the current crisis. such consolidation should not, however, impede competition to such an extent as to impair the proper functioning of the common market. the control of concentrations carried out for this purpose is the responsibility of the commission under the relevant community regulation or of the competent national authorities, depending on the size of the concentration concerned.

i therefore think we have taken a balanced approach on this issue. it was important that we found a way to label our wines to make sure that consumers knew what they bought, and with the pdos you have the possibility today of putting information on the label to inform the consumer that this is a wine produced by the traditional method. i said in my first speech that i met some wine producers from provence. they did not like ‘traditional rosé’ exactly for the reason you said – that it was a bit old fashioned. i then asked for new ideas on another way of labelling that it was the traditional method, but i have had no suggestions on this issue. the idea is to make it optional for member states whether they make it compulsory within their territory for producers to put on the label whether it is a coupage or a traditional product. we will have this discussion again in the regulatory committee. as i said, a vote will probably take place on 19 june 2009 and then we will look into the outcome of this vote, in which member states will represent the opinions of their governments. that will be interesting, but i am still quite convinced that what we have proposed, which is in compliance with the oiv oenological practices, will be the way forward for our wine producers to stay competitive on the global market.

on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, i am one of those curious constitutional animals who think that the rules of procedure mirror our own work here in our own parliament. so i think this is an important piece of work by richard corbett, who is the eminent specialist in this field. to his credit i must say that this is the second modification of the rules of procedure. there are things that have changed very recently and that we are changing again, because we have seen that the practice of our parliament demands that those changes be made. i have one general remark and three specific ones to put before you tonight. the general remark is that i think it is also very important to talk about the second aspect of mr corbett’s report: the impact of the lisbon treaty upon our rules of procedure. it is very important to talk about that too, because a change to the rules of procedure without that second part would be imperfect. my three specific remarks: the first one concerns a modification in which i played a small part myself. it is the fact that we are trying to put into the rules of procedure this idea of an agora, of the possibility for citizens, too, to address the european parliament and to participate in discussions before the european parliament. i think this is a symbolically important initiative that we took together with my friend and colleague gérard onesta, and i think it would be a good thing if that were to be enshrined in the rules of procedure. the second thing that is, i think, of importance is the change we have made to the own-initiative reports: the fact that, having seen how the own-initiative reports have been in practice, we are reintroducing the possibility for amendments, albeit by one 10th of parliamentarians. the third aspect madam president, the five billion euro programme for economic recovery is intended to exploit the capitalist crisis and to facilitate the achievement of capital's objectives by promoting capitalist restructuring in strategic sectors such as energy and electronic communications. broadband and networks are essential for rural development but are not a priority. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new measures, such as the introduction of a new system of quotas for the production of milk and milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk and milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk and milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk and milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk and milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products, the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products, and the introduction of a new system of quotas for the marketing of milk products. the one and a half billion euro is essentially earmarked for the development of electronic communications companies and not for agriculture and rural development. the same applies to the three and a half billion allocated to completing the single electricity grid and the single electricity market, facilitating the privatisations, mergers and acquisitions promoted by the third liberalisation package and the seizure and storage of coal - this very expensive and unenvironmental project aimed at increasing profits and continuing pollution from power stations. the workers and farmers understand that these measures are taken to strengthen capital and monopolies, so they reject them and fight them by demanding the satisfaction of popular changes so that they do not become survivors of the crisis.
the report and the initiative we are discussing today are necessary, but i fear that they are not very effective. it's more of an injection than a cure. europe had to do something because the crisis is big - it is important that it did something - but i think it is not up to the challenge. firstly, there is little money for the crisis we are going through and there is no guarantee that it will reach those who need it most. the sectors are important and energy and broadband, but it is not certain that they are the first priority sectors and especially those that will produce more jobs and more growth as we need. secondly, as all my colleagues have said, a large part of the money - almost half of it - 2.4 billion, we do not yet know if, when and where it will come from. the other day we were discussing in the committee on budgets with commissioner kallas, who is not in a position to give us an answer at the moment, where the appropriations will come from. thirdly, and perhaps most importantly, we are giving solutions that are not helping us in the long run to deal with the problems. it is not the solution, ladies and gentlemen, to keep taking money from the margins of agricultural policy, it is not the solution to change the balance between rural and regional policy and the other needs of the union underground. the european union must be able to respond to the crisis with a comprehensive plan, which is not in sight at the moment. i am afraid that this opportunity has been lost for the european union and i am afraid that it has been lost above all for the commission itself.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. firstly, the undeniable need to do more in europe for energy interconnections and broadband. secondly, our debate today indirectly raises the very important question of the present and future of agricultural expenditure from the community budget. the positive message, of course, is that europe is using the community budget as a tool against the crisis. that's a positive and we're sticking to it. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the financing of the common agricultural policy, which is the subject of a proposal from the commission, is a very good one, and i think that it is a good one. but be careful! if this systemic approach leads us to a simplistic, deductive reasoning that agriculture will always have unallocated funds to meet new needs, and from this we conclude that agriculture already has far more than it needs - in view of the important debates on the future of agriculture after 2013 - this would be a major strategic mistake for europe. so he should not feel that we need to go "lower" in agriculture because, as the budget has shown, before 2013 we always had money, which we took from here to make them galileo, to make them energy, to make them broadband. agriculture needs resources and will need resources beyond 2013. at the same time, we must also make clear in the european union what is self-evident: the commission's proposals are based on the principle of subsidiarity.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: mr president, the present economic crisis is a crisis of overproduction and over-accumulation of capital. it is, as everyone now admits, a crisis of the capitalist system itself. the attempts to present this crisis as a financial crisis, as a liquidity crisis, are being made to disorient workers and to prevent them from recognizing the real causes that lead to the increase in unemployment, the decrease in incomes, the elasticity of labour relations and the all-out attack on them. the accounting standards supervision measures with credit and equity checks taken not only do not limit the banks' solvency, but are in fact measures that the banks themselves seek in order to ensure this solvency under a guise of supervision and control. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the banking system is not under threat from the economic crisis, and that the banking system is not under threat from the economic crisis. the reluctance of the banks to even co-finance themselves with the public in return for even minimal controls is characteristic of their attitude. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the workers are not fooled or misled by the european union's decisions to exit the crisis. they know that these decisions are putting the burden of the crisis on their shoulders and are aimed at securing even greater profits for capital.

president-in-office of the council. − the council has not received any proposal from the commission for a european employment tax. therefore the council is not in a position to answer the specific points raised by the honourable member on this issue. however, the council shares the concerns expressed by the honourable member in her question and considers that it is important to preserve social cohesion and to promote the growth and economic recovery of the european union, which is suffering from the effects of the global crisis. in this framework, the presidency would like to recall that member states remain, in the first instance, responsible for designing and implementing the employment policies. nevertheless, the council pays special attention to these employment policies, especially now that europe is facing a financial economic crisis and adopts new early employment guidelines in accordance with the treaty. in this sense, the presidency would like to recall that in december last year the european council decided an overall european economic recovery plan to address, inter alia, employment issues resulting from the financial crisis. the plan consists of immediate budgetary measures amounting to eur 200 billion comprising, on the one hand, community measures amounting to eur 30 billion and, on the other hand, national measures that would amount to eur 170 billion. the european council also supported the idea of rapid action by the european social fund in support of employment, especially for the benefit of the most vulnerable groups in the population, such as supporting flexicurity policies and policies easing job transition periods, and giving member states the opportunity, where necessary, for reprogramming european social fund expenditures in order to strengthen their employment strategies. it is also to be recalled that, in addition to the european social fund, the european globalisation adjustment fund also provides community assistance to complement national actions, including actions at regional and local levels. this fund, created by the council in 2007, addresses specific european-scale crises caused by globalisation and provides one-off, time-limited individual support geared directly to redundant workers. a revision of the european globalisation adjustment fund is under way and the european council of march 2009 called for a swift agreement to be reached. the council welcomes the agreement for a first-reading adoption of this revision and goes by today’s vote in plenary. the european council of march also agreed upon some additional measures such as the following: removing barriers while preventing the creation of new ones and achieving a fully operational internal market; further reducing administrative burdens; improving framework conditions for industry in order to maintain a strong industrial base and for businesses with a special focus on smes and innovation; encouraging partnership between business, research, education and training; and stepping up and improving the quality of investment in research, knowledge, and education. finally the presidency would like to recall that the initiative of the current presidency, an employment summit, will be organised on 7 may in prague. yesterday the czech deputy prime minister for european affairs, mr vondra, had the opportunity to speak at this plenary on this issue on behalf of the presidency.
