the committee on international trade has adopted an opinion on the proposal. - the mr president, as the author of the opinion of the committee on international trade, i would like to point out that the changes introduced by the treaty of lisbon in the field of the common commercial policy contribute, as a whole, to strengthening the democratic legitimacy, transparency and effectiveness of the union's external action. i would particularly like to stress the need to redress the institutional balance within the union by making the european parliament a co-legislator in defining the framework for the implementation of the common commercial policy. parliament's approval will also be required for the conclusion of all trade agreements. i would like to point out, however, the imbalance between the internal and external powers of parliament - in foro interno / in foro externo if you will - in the common commercial policy, since the treaty of lisbon does not give parliament the right to approve the commission's mandate for negotiating trade agreements. i believe that parliament is nevertheless entitled to set conditions for giving its consent to the conclusion of trade agreements of the union. i think that a strengthened framework agreement on relations between parliament and the european commission is necessary in this respect. finally, i would like to stress the need for a strengthened dialogue between the european parliament and the national parliaments, since all matters relating to the common commercial policy will be the exclusive competence of the union. all trade agreements will be union agreements and there will no longer be mixed agreements concluded by both the union and the member states.
mr president, it is indeed a very solemn day: the lisbon package, as it is called, is finally being presented to parliament. the czech senate gave a sign of hope; many are making their final speeches, the emotion is palpable, we are closing this parliament; many of us are quite moved. there is a real historic atmosphere in this evening's sitting of parliament. as a member of the european parliament, i would like to speak today, as a half-constitutionalist, half-budgetary janus, a little more specifically about the guy-quint report on the new budgetary system and the impact that the lisbon treaty will have on this new system. first of all, it is a more democratic system - as has been said. from now on all expenditure, the budget as a whole, is established by the co-decision procedure between the council and parliament. it is also - and this is even more important - a more political budget, since we have - as mrs guy-quint calls it - interinstitutional strategic programming, that is to say, all the bodies of the european union agree that this budget should be made. but it is a system that still has uncertainties. for example, will parliament really play its role, which is a priori strengthened? will he benefit from this new power, given that there are also problems? we have less time. there's only one reading. it is therefore up to parliament to seize this challenge and this opportunity to play its part. will the budgetary five-year period coincide with or overlap with the parliamentary five-year period? it's not safe. here, too, effort is required. there are also missed opportunities. we have lost the opportunity to have increased own resources, we have lost the opportunity - give me another ten seconds, since we are in a solemn and nocturnal session - to implement a new budgetary philosophy. finally, i would like to stress that we are faced with challenges: the challenge of transition - it is not easy to switch from one system to another from one moment to the next - and the challenge of flexibility - we need more flexibility to be able to cope with crises. i would like to conclude by expressing the hope that all this will be developed further with the implementation of the lisbon treaty.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the commission's proposal for the media mondus programme is welcomed by all those who want to see the european audiovisual sector develop, become ever more powerful and competitive, and also exported to the rest of the world. the european audiovisual industry has developed and improved significantly in recent years and its international presence has been transformed over the last twenty years, in particular due to technological developments. in some markets this has led to strong economic growth, investment and therefore increased demand for audiovisual material. unfortunately, however, there are obstacles affecting the circulation of european works on foreign markets - among them the inadequate financing of european audiovisual companies. community support for the audiovisual sector takes account of the fact that the european union and its member states favour cooperation with third countries and the competent international organisations in the cultural field, because it emphasises the importance of respecting the different cultural dimensions in order to promote their diversity and because the distribution sector determines the diversity of audiovisual works and consumer choices. the circulation of european audiovisual works on the international market remains limited, while audiovisual works from third countries - other than the united states - face similar circulation problems on european markets. european distributors are basically small companies with limited equipment to gain access to international markets. the new programme therefore provides resources to take measures to improve the distribution, circulation and promotion of european audiovisual works in third countries and, by extension, third countries in europe. finally, i would like to congratulate my colleague ruth hieronymi on another excellent job and to wish her a good career in society and her role in the future after her excellent presence in the european parliament. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank commissioner reding and all the colleagues on the committee on culture, education, media, sport and multilingualism for the good cooperation we have had in this five-year term.
the next item is the debate on six motions for resolutions on iran: the case of roxana saberi.
member of the commission. − madam president, the commission is following closely the case of ms roxana saberi, including the preparations for the appeal process that will begin early next week. the commission is concerned about ms saberi’s health, weakened by a hunger strike that, according to ms saberi’s father, she started after she was sentenced to eight years in jail for alleged espionage by the revolutionary court of tehran last month. the commission considers that ms saberi’s trial, held behind closed doors, did not meet the minimum standards required for a fair and transparent trial. the commission fully supported the declaration issued by the czech presidency of the council on ms saberi’s case on 22 april 2009. we hope that the iranian judiciary will ensure a fair and transparent appeal trial without any delay, with all the guarantees provided by the iranian legislation. the commission is deeply concerned at the steady deterioration of the situation of human rights and fundamental freedoms in iran. the recent execution of ms delara darabi, hanged on 1 may for a crime she allegedly committed when she was a minor, is another confirmation of this dismal situation. also in this case the eu issued a statement strongly condemning the execution. the commission has repeatedly urged, and will continue to do so, the iranian authorities to respect their international commitments on human rights, including in relation to the international covenant on civil and political rights and the universal declaration on human rights. the improvement of the human rights situation in iran is, for the commission, essential to enhance its political dialogue and cooperation with iran in the near future.

the next item is the debate on six motions for resolutions on madagascar. the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos.
member of the commission. − madam president, firstly i would like to underline the commission’s great concern at the current volatile situation in madagascar. i would also like to stress the commission’s continued commitment to the malagasy people. the situation in that country after the forced removal of president ravalomanana on 17 march deserves and requires our full attention and, like the european parliament, the commission is following events very closely. the commission has fully endorsed the czech presidency statement issued on behalf of the european union on 20 march condemning the transfer of power and calling on the malagasy parties to comply fully with the provisions of the constitution of madagascar. the commission considers that there has been a flagrant violation of essential elements of the cotonou agreement and that this is a ‘case of special urgency’ within the meaning of article 96 of that agreement. the commission has therefore launched the procedure to propose to the council to open consultation with the authorities in power in order to examine possible solutions to the crisis, aimed at the re-establishment of constitutional order. the commission will continue to use all the dialogue means it has at its disposal to find an overall solution to the current crisis. to that end, it is enhancing the political dialogue, based on article 8 of the cotonou agreement, with all the relevant stakeholders in madagascar. it is also participating in the main international efforts deployed, notably in the framework of the international contact group set up recently by the african union. at this stage, the prevailing view is that the relevant malagasy political stakeholders agree on a road map for a return to constitutional order and the holding of elections.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place after the debate.

after being charged with corruption in venezuela. the commission has taken note of the explanation provided by the president of rosales’ party, according to which he has gone into hiding for his own sectors of venezuelan society have criticised the proliferation of measures which they consider adversely affect the right to freedom of expression and the freedom to exercise political rights. this same sector considers that the government is showing an intolerant attitude towards criticism. we are conscious of these facts and we are closely following the political situation in venezuela. i would like to underline the importance that the european union attaches to freedom of expression and opinion, a fundamental human right and a cornerstone of democracy and the rule of law. we hope that the democratic institutions of venezuela will respect the rule of law and preserve democracy in the country, whilst complying with the obligation arising from the international agreements signed and ratified by venezuela, including the american convention on human rights and, specifically, the provisions on political rights set out in article 23. i wish to assure parliament that the commission will continue to follow developments in venezuela closely. the commission’s commitment to supporting and strengthening democracy and the protection and promotion of human rights and fundamental freedoms will continue to be reflected in our cooperation policies and relations with venezuela.

the swedish presidency's programme, mr president, does indeed include all the important issues of our time: realities and the future. i'll start with the financial crisis. i think that the swedish presidency should look into this matter more deeply. the social market economy needs investment in the real economy and not just in financial products like the ones that have led us to where we are today. the climate and sustainable development, including social development, and the labour market are certainly important issues. but here too, we need real investment in the economy and more control. a market economy that tries to turn to laissez-faire is not what we need in europe. the problems of crime, home affairs and justice really concern us all. it is clear that organised crime is not what it used to be and it is clear that it is linked to terrorism and all kinds of crime, so different approaches are needed. i think that what is more important is to see also, in relation to the immigration of large numbers of third country nationals, whether the union's development policy has really succeeded and in what areas. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to support them. the swedish presidency's programme is indeed very ambitious, particularly in the transatlantic dialogue. the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome, and it is not a party to the treaty of rome.
in writing the priorities of the swedish presidency signal an even greater intensity of the eu's anti-people offensive aimed at ensuring the continued profitability of eu capital by burdening the working class and the popular strata with the burdens of the capitalist economic crisis. the swedish presidency is seeking to accelerate capitalist restructuring in the context of the lisbon strategy. at the heart of the anti-working class attack is the reduction of wages and pensions, the complete demolition of labour relations, workers' rights, social protection and insurance systems, the even greater commercialisation of health and education. the 'green economy' is designed to open up new areas of activity which are profitable for capital under the pretext of 'combating climate change'. the 'stockholm programme' is attempting - in the name of fighting 'terrorism and organised crime' - to further arm the bourgeois political system in order to counter popular reactions, to intensify repressive measures against migrants. with the baltic strategy, it is initiating a greater expansion of the eu monopolies in the countries on the eu's eastern border, seeking to strengthen their position in imperialist competition. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

member of the commission. - the madam president, i would first of all like to express my sympathy to those affected by the recent forest fires. this summer the community made a major contribution to the fight against the many and difficult forest fires which have broken out in several member states. the pilot programme of the european union's regular european fire reserve played an important role in providing assistance to member states affected by forest fires. i would like to thank the european parliament in particular for approving the financing of this pilot programme. i should also mention that this pilot project is a small part, a partial implementation of the proposals and ideas in the report by michel barnier, to whom i also express my thanks. this pilot programme has substantially strengthened the community's civil protection mechanism and, consequently, the ability to contribute to countries affected by natural disasters. through the european civil protection mechanism, france, italy, spain, and other european union member states have provided aerial firefighting equipment to greece, italy, portugal, and other countries. for the first time, two community firefighting aircraft took part, together with greek, portuguese and french aircraft, in the firefighting effort in the framework of a pilot programme to create an autonomous community firefighting force. the recent fires have again prompted many voices from politicians, scientists, journalists and ordinary citizens to call for the creation of a specialised firefighting force at european level, which can intervene immediately and effectively when national firefighting resources are inadequate. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it has enabled us to secure funds for the chartering of firefighting aircraft and, for the first time this summer, we set up and put into operation, from 1 june to 30 september, an air firefighting force under the supervision of the european commission - the european forest fire reserves - whose aircraft have participated in firefighting operations in the various countries, in particular in six of the nine cases in which the european union's civil protection mechanism was called upon to assist. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a step in the right direction, and it is a step which we must take to ensure that the directive is implemented in a way that is compatible with the objectives of the directive. the pilot programme is intended to fill any gaps in the air capacity of the member states and not to replace the available national means. the chartered spare aircraft were used this year, as i said, in portugal, in the south of france and corsica, in italy and in greece, in the region of attica, where we had the big fires. they are based on the island of corsica, which is equidistant from both geographical ends of the mediterranean. i believe that the need to establish an autonomous capacity to deal with natural disasters at community level is now urgent. we hope that this pilot programme will form the nucleus of a future european rapid reaction force to combat forest fires or other natural or man-made disasters. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's forestry sector is not affected by the fires. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the management of forests, including the establishment of a european forestry area. however, there are also reservations about the conferral of further powers on the commission in the field of civil protection. it is also argued that the existence of a european firefighting force would give a false sense of security to many national authorities, which might neglect the necessary investments required in human resources, means of fighting and, above all, in the prevention of fires or other natural disasters. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's forestry policy is not disrupted by the fires. for example, on the basis of which criteria and priorities will european aerial firefighting units be determined and used in the event of a fire being carried out in several countries at the same time, e.g. in portugal and greece at the same time. all these issues will, however, be analysed in a report to be submitted by the european commission for discussion by the council of ministers and the european parliament on the operation of the pilot programme this summer. the council and the european parliament and the commission must be convinced and cooperate in the creation of this mechanism. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i would like to say a few words about the civil protection mechanism which was set up in 2001 and which in its early years operated with little intervention. since 2005, we have increased our interventions fivefold, both inside and outside the european union: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and it's well known how important the response was to both the tsunami and hurricane katrina. in fact, in both cases we were the first to send experts to the disaster sites. but as i said before, we have a lot to do, and anyone who reads the barnier report will see how far we can take civil protection to the benefit of european citizens and of course to the protection of the environment.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the forests in the south of europe are protected. this is a disaster of great magnitude and systematicity. in the coming decades the problem will get worse. for at least the next 30 to 40 years, whatever we do to reduce greenhouse gases, we will be experiencing a climate deterioration which, particularly in southern europe where forests are vulnerable, will be felt negatively. we're going to live in a future with bigger fires and worse dangers. under normal circumstances, firefighting is a responsibility and an obligation of the member states. and the member states in southern europe are successfully dealing with tens of thousands of fires a year. but every now and then, in exceptional circumstances, there are fires of great devastation where external aid and solidarity are needed. the draft decision which we are proposing on behalf of the european people's party is based on these findings and highlights, among other things, the urgency of the creation of an effective european force for immediate intervention, to which commissioner dimas referred and which is proposed in the barnier report. stresses the need for flexible and immediate mobilisation of the european solidarity fund, the need to give priority to tackling forest fires in southern europe in the union's action plan for adaptation to climate change currently under preparation, and the need to submit the commission's proposal for a european policy on natural disaster response as soon as possible; it is important for my country, and above all for the citizens affected, that this decision be voted on by the european parliament. it is important that the house should show its sensitivity to this crucial issue for southern europe.
on behalf of the s-d group. madam president, commissioner, this year, for another year, the history of forest fires is repeated, it is getting worse, and we come every autumn, after the holidays, to discuss the rehabilitation of the affected areas and the relief of the victims. in greece we are again mourning this year hundreds of thousands of hectares of burnt land, an immense ecological and economic disaster, and the same extent of destruction is in spain, portugal, italy and france. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. for at least six years we have been talking about a common european civil protection force, a constant demand of the european parliament, but whose importance is being undermined by the mistakes of certain national governments. errors in the design and implementation of preventive mechanisms and measures, failure or reluctance to enforce strict legislation making the reforestation of damaged areas compulsory, failure to use all available community resources to rehabilitate those affected. at community level, in addition to the civil protection force, the immediate mobilisation of the funds of the solidarity fund and, above all, its release from bureaucratic mechanisms must be the commission's main priorities. finally, commissioner, you know better than we do that we are facing a new generation of fires, the so-called high-intensity fires, which are directly linked to climate change and are affecting the forests of the mediterranean and southern europe in particular. we therefore urgently need to redesign forest protection at national and community level by adapting fire prevention and response to new data and integrating these policies into the ongoing measures to adapt member states to the challenge of climate change.

who are speaking on this very important subject which concerns the whole of the european south, since large areas are regularly destroyed by huge forest fires and the quality of life, biodiversity, regional development and the future of the citizens are degraded. often, forest fires, as recently in greece, are due to inadequate and conflicting forestry and housing policies that favour arson and illegal actions aimed at the disposal of forest land. unfortunately, both major parties have tried to water down, to reduce the constitutional protection of forests. the continuing pressures for land-use change and settlement expansion and, of course, uncontrolled landfills that burn or self-ignite, coupled with climate change, which for some is just an alibi, are now increasing the chances of more natural disasters. it is therefore important to focus more on prevention, protection and early response to risks at a european level through coordinated and cooperative actions. in this context, we must ensure the implementation of a common european sustainable forestry policy and ensure that the money from the cohesion funds for regional development and social solidarity, which is earmarked for risk prevention and management and for the rehabilitation of affected areas, is actually used in a rational and sustainable way. the funds allocated to the rehabilitation of affected areas must be used on the basis of rational reforestation measures and scientific studies, and we stress that they must be reimbursed by the member states when it is established that deforestation of affected areas has been carried out for the benefit of housing or tourism. in the past, and only two years ago, parliament had made recommendations in resolutions on the prevention and immediate response to natural hazards, which, unfortunately, have not been implemented effectively. we believe that the long-term protection of forests and biodiversity is only possible through the implementation of sustainable risk prevention and management policies at national, regional and local level with the active participation of local communities, the containment of the rural population and the creation of new 'green' professions, lifelong education and training, the strengthening of forest services with emphasis on volunteer forest firefighting teams. finally, we must intensify our efforts to develop more effective mechanisms for responding to natural disasters in a timely manner by strengthening the permanent civil protection force mentioned by mr dimas and by further exploiting the conclusions of the barnier report. in this context, we are today tabled a resolution in the european parliament in the hope that we will make some progress in terms of the partnership and cooperation of the states for an effective prevention and response to natural disasters, and the more efficient use of european union funds for this purpose. we owe it to our kids.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the fires in greece, and i would like to thank the commissioner for his report. the 2007 fires, among the worst such tragedies, and this year's fires in northeastern attica, have exposed the nakedness of the fire and environmental protection system. i would remind you that athens is the european capital with the lowest proportion of green space per capita, the highest air pollution and that the fires of 2007 and 2009 have worsened the environmental problem. perhaps climate change can explain to some extent the disasters we have in southern europe. but there are shortcomings in both national policies and in the european union's policy to the extent that it has not adequately integrated climate change into the environmental strategy. the greek governments tolerate the burning of areas and the expropriation of public property, while the european union funds are being used in a rigid manner. i would therefore ask you, commissioner, to consider: does the european commission intend to contribute to the institutional protection of the country's forests by immediately initiating the process of drawing up and ratifying forest maps which, if they had existed, might have prevented these fires? will it immediately co-finance flood control and erosion control projects to prevent further disasters in the affected areas? does it intend to finance the appropriate reforestation, because it is not being done to a sufficient extent to compensate for the damage? finally, does the european commission intend to discuss with the new government to be elected in greece the creation of a metropolitan park at the former airport of eliniko, an act which will breathe life into attica? i would like to thank you very much and wish you a happy birthday on the occasion of the commissioner's name day, as we say in greece!

member of the commission. − the commission shares the concern of the honourable member that population growth in africa and the long-term impact of high fertility rates could contribute significantly to increased pressure on africa’s natural resources and determining the development trajectory of the african continent. fertility rates tell an important part of the story; according to the un population division, africa’s overall population today is 8% lower than if its fertility rate had stayed at its 1970 levels. indeed its fertility rate is expected to decline even below 2.5% by 2050. in urban parts of the continent an emergent middle class is having fewer children at rates comparable with europeans. this story is one of promise, of the countries which have attained political stability and achieved impressive economic growth. aware of these challenges, the european commission has a development policy geared to fight poverty, promote sustainable development and tackle political challenges in order to help foster stability. in this field the commission is also bound by the strategy endorsed by the 1994 international conference on population and development, further reviewed in 1999. the strategy has extended the concept of family planning to that of sexual and reproductive health and rights. it emphasises human rights, the empowerment of women, the importance of investing in health and education and provision of comprehensive reproductive health services to all those who need them. in particular the education of women has an impact on their reproductive behaviour. many studies have found a strong correlation between education and fertility; as literacy improves fertility rates tend to decrease. across all of its programmes the commission expects to commit around eur 1.7 billion to education for the years 2007-2013; more generally, we are firmly engaged in increasing the level and effectiveness of the collective eu aid to health systems delivering universal coverage of basic services including reproductive health. in this respect, under the eu agenda for action on mdgs – millennium development goals – the eu has undertaken to contribute an additional eur 8 billion, of which eur 6 billion for africa, to health, providing that all aid commitments are fully met. in order to address environmental pressure, it is of key importance to ensure that local livelihoods are sustainable. this means combating desertification and land degradation and enhancing agricultural productivity, halting over-exploitation of biodiversity, forests and other natural resources including oceans and inland waters; and, finally, ensuring that climate change remains within certain limits and assisting african populations to adapt to climate change. the commission, together with the eu member states, is working on an environmental integration strategy to ensure that development cooperation efforts contribute to these objectives. our preparations for the copenhagen climate change summit are to be seen in this light. at present the eu is working with the african union and other regional organisations to strengthen their capacity to address environment and climate change issues. it promotes important initiatives to enhance forest governance, especially through forest law enforcement, governance and trade.
member of the commission. − we do not have a specific family planning policy, but this can and does happen at the request of the governments concerned. a lot of maternal deaths result from abortions in unacceptable circumstances. in countries where the legislation allows abortion, the commission will also support these programmes. so whether or not we take such measures really depends on the countries concerned. with respect to the second question; i must say that when you look at the african continent and the birth rates, as i explained in my introduction, there is a clear relationship between economic development, the degree of urbanisation and fertility rates. this is not a new phenomenon. we have seen that in all countries all over the globe. one can expect, with global urbanisation and, hopefully, increased growth figures, that the fertility rates might go down. this is not limited, as the honourable member is suggesting, to some countries. it is rather a phenomenon that is linked to the development of the country concerned.
member of the commission. − the protection of human rights is of the highest priority in the european union’s relations with colombia. we are therefore following the situation in colombia very closely. we are well aware of the difficulties confronting trade unionism in colombia, and the continued killings of, and threats against, union leaders and members. we know this from our information sources, from the reports and statements issued by international treaty bodies, as well as from my own discussions with bodies such as the european trade union confederation. serious concerns remain concerning the effective application of the ilo core conventions in the country. we are consistently urging the government to step up efforts to protect the most vulnerable population groups and to investigate and punish all human rights violations. recent attacks on human rights defenders and trade unionists have been the subject of démarches by the eu troika ambassadors in bogotá and were also raised during recent high-level meetings between european union and colombian officials. moreover, we have recently launched a bilateral human rights dialogue with the colombian government which provides a channel for a more regular and systematic exchange of information and experience in the human rights field, and will help inform technical cooperation. furthermore, we are seeking to include additional guarantees in the planned multiparty trade agreement in order to improve implementation of core labour and environmental conventions in colombia as part of the sustainable development chapter. we are also proposing monitoring by civil society institutions on the implementation of labour laws. we hope that in this manner the agreement will help to improve the situation for labour rights activists in colombia.

acting president of the council. - we discussed this earlier today. during the current crisis, public authorities have taken extraordinary measures, both in terms of monetary policy and of budget support. these efforts have been necessary and appropriate and have played an important role in avoiding a more serious crisis, stabilising the economy and preventing a dramatic downturn. however, the stabilisation of the economic and financial situation does not mean that the recession is over. we must be very careful to ensure that our policy for the future combines the necessary support for the recovery - which we hope will come - with a responsible approach to the sustainability of public finances in the medium and long term. it is precisely the need to balance these two objectives that makes the flexibility actually allowed in the 2005 revision of the pact so sensible. in difficult economic times, public authorities must take the necessary measures to promote economic activity. however, once the recovery has been accelerated and driven forward on our own, we must ensure that our public finances are sustainable and establish credible plans to consolidate our budgets. given the very different circumstances in the different member states, there will be different plans, different timetables, for ending fiscal stimulus and monetary adjustment. this will be reflected in many of the deficit procedures that will be addressed to individual member states, but they will need to be coordinated and will continue to be consistent with the overall framework of the stability and growth pact. the presidency is therefore planning discussions in ecofin in the autumn on appropriate exit strategies and their coordination. i expect that these discussions will lead to a clear consensus on ambitious consolidation at the right time in each member state to ensure long-term sustainability.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i too would like to express my deep regret at the humanitarian disaster in taiwan, and i really believe that we as the european union should help in any way we can. we now know that 640 people have died from hurricane marrakech and more than 192 are missing. a landslide, in fact, wiped an entire village off the map and led to hundreds of deaths. but all of this is not accidental. it was preceded by extensive dehydration, which reduced the earth's ability to absorb rain. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to finance the necessary measures. a hurricane is formed in sea areas with a surface temperature of 27 degrees celsius. rising temperatures will significantly increase both hurricanes and their intensity. as of today, 120 million people live in areas affected by hurricanes. between 1980 and 2000, we had 250,000 hurricane victims. for all these reasons, our hearts must be in copenhagen, where in december we will decide whether each of our countries will stand up for its own temporary, short-term interests, or whether we will all fight for the planet - our home - the fight to reverse climate change. so will the developed and developing world stop contributing to disasters like this one in taiwan? but that requires action. it requires us to commit to making the necessary commitments to the necessary reductions, so that the temperature does not rise above 2 degrees celsius, it also requires us to put our hand in the pocket to help the developing world financially to be part of the solution and to help tackle climate change. my dear colleagues, it is we, the developing world, who have caused climate change. we are the polluter and according to the principle we ourselves have established - 'the polluter pays' - we must pay. we must pay for it by giving economic aid to developing countries.
president-in-office of the council. − mr president, i see that the debate from the irish referendum still lives in this plenary and there is a lot to say about democracy. is it democratic to let the irish people vote once again? well yes, it is. it is very democratic. if you listen to the people, you ask them: why did you vote ‘no’? you find out the reasons. you clarify these things by giving legal guarantees to the irish people on taxation, on neutrality, on certain ethical issues and also making a guarantee that all member states, including ireland, will have a commissioner when the new treaty comes into force. with this clarification you have a new referendum. the people answered ‘yes’ with a two-thirds majority and with a higher turnout than last time. this is democratic, and i think we should rejoice and congratulate the irish people for this. i also see that there is a clear domestic debate from the uk in here; it would be tempting to go into that, but i will not do so. i just want to say that, independently of who is in downing street next year, britain needs europe and europe does need britain. i also sense a certain frustration in here about the timetable now. i can understand that and i share it, but i want to assure the house that we move as swiftly as we can. we have guarantees or promises that the polish president will sign within a few days. the timetable in prague is still a little bit unclear. i will go there tonight, if i catch the plane, and will meet with lots of people tomorrow in order to get a clearer picture of the different scenarios, the different timetables that we can expect. we need a few days to grasp and to assess the situation in the constitutional court. the treaty is in the constitutional court and, until it has left there, the president cannot sign. i am quite optimistic: i think this will be ready within a short time, but we need to wait a few days for this clarification. meanwhile, the presidency is working, of course. there are lots of working groups going on in order to prepare for the full implementation of the treaty. there are discussions going on with the european parliament, with the commission, in order to put everything in place that we need to do so that the treaty can enter into force very swiftly. i received a question from madame flautre about the extra meps; that is an issue that will have to be dealt with as soon as the treaty enters into force, and i want to assure her, if she is listening now, that we are doing whatever we can to ensure that the decision can be made as soon as possible. i want to thank parliament for having decided to give these meps observer status while we wait for the formalities once the treaty is adopted. she also named many men who are being mentioned for the top jobs, but those are men named in the media, in the press; there are no official candidates from the council yet. there will be. but all those names mentioned are named in the media. i would very much welcome if one of those top jobs could go to a woman. i cannot guarantee you that we will succeed with that because a presidency has to listen to all the capitals and find candidates who can achieve a consensus among the 27 member states, but i would very much welcome the candidacy of a woman; it would make europe much more representative than today. i also feel, mr president, a very strong sensation in this house that europe should move on to be a stronger, bigger player in the world and to show resolve when it comes to the economy, the fight against unemployment, tackling the challenges of globalisation and the climate issue. we need to do that. the lisbon treaty is an important tool for us to do so; but we also need to move on independently of what treaty we have, to show concrete results and to deliver. only when we do that, when we deliver what the citizens expect of us – and this goes for the council, the commission and the european parliament – will we gain legitimacy and the confidence of our citizens. i can assure you that the presidency is doing everything it can to move the processes on on all these issues, with the help of the european parliament. thank you very much for an interesting debate, mr president.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, it has been an interesting debate, which i am sure to some extent repeats the discussions that have been held in ireland and elsewhere. could i just start with some facts. it is the member states’ governments who decide on the method for ratification. there is no way that the eu institutions can force a member state to choose either a referendum or a parliamentary ratification. i think it is very important to say this. it is interesting that those who speak very strongly in favour of independent nation states are also the ones who would be willing to impose a referendum on all other member states, which i find a bit strange. now ireland decided to carry out a second referendum. let us be very honest, this always carries a political risk, but it was the irish government which decided that they were willing to do so. and why did they do that? incidentally, it is not unusual to repeat referenda: that has been done previously on domestic issues, and in certain member states you can come back several times on a domestic issue too, so let us be intellectually honest about this whole procedure. i think we have heard a very good analysis from the irish meps themselves, from mr kelly, from mary macdonald, from mr de rossa: they offered an explanation on why there was a change among the irish population, on why they moved towards a ‘yes’ vote, and i think we have had good answers. i think some meps have a very strange way of looking at democracy as very static and absolute. they maybe prefer to forget that this is a discussion about a reform. it is a process that started in laeken many years ago and has involved different democratic bodies and discussions over the years to try to find a common way of taking decisions in a more modern, more democratic and efficient way. member states and their leaders have of course invested a lot of time and energy in this procedure and this is why it is not static: it cannot be compared with a football match. i am sorry, you cannot count scores in the same way as in a football match, because we also have to listen to each other. and this is what was done. the concerns of the irish people were listened to and they did it themselves in ireland. why do we have to explain? the irish themselves, their national parliament, debated in a subcommittee the reasons why there was a ‘no’ vote – because this came as a surprise to many there. so they looked at the reasons. i was there myself. i was at the fashion fair in dublin, i was at the fish market in cork, i was at the public meeting in donegal, and what many people said first of all was: well, we have not actually read the full text of the treaty, it is a very complex legal text and it is difficult to understand exactly what this is all about. some said they were very afraid that what was on the posters might be true, that the eu will decide on a minimum wage which is eur 1.48: can that really be true? or is it true that the eu will impose conscription to a european army and send very young people to afghanistan in a european army: can this be true? what kind of statements are these? should i believe in them? so there were a lot of worries and a lot of real concerns and mainly, i would say, a lack of information and a need for their concerns to be taken seriously. this is what happened. civil society also engaged, as i think mr kelly explained very well. i am not ashamed of the fact that we made a citizens’ summary of the lisbon treaty which was then published in the big newspapers – uncontested by anybody i may add – so that citizens could themselves read a summary in understandable language of the full treaty, so that they could judge by themselves what was in there, what was true or not. i think the legal guarantees helped, because then it was clarified that they did not have to worry about neutrality or abortion or any of the other concerns. so this was clarified and they got legal guarantees and they got a commissioner – and thank you, ireland, because it means that there will be a swedish commissioner also from now on, also a german one, and one from greece etc., so i think we also have to thank the irish for setting that straight. of course, we do not debate these things in a political vacuum. the reality will also influence how we think about these issues, and there is nothing wrong with that. but i think we should always, in the analysis that follows after a referendum, think very carefully about any fear factors on both sides, because fear is a very strong emotion and it might be misused. so i think that the debate that will follow in ireland should also address how to avoid using or misusing any fear factor. but the reality showed that the irish people believe that they have a place in the centre of europe, and that they will be helped by belonging fully to europe and not being questioned about whether this is true or not. i also hope that the commission can continue to play this role of providing factual objective information, but the debate will go on because, remember, the upside of a referendum is that you have to engage with citizens. you have to provide information and a debate and a discussion. the downside is that it also divides the population. you are forced to say ‘yes’ or ‘no’ and that can stay for a long time in the hearts and minds of the irish as well. we have a duty, an obligation democratically, to also take the concerns of the ‘no’ side seriously, to continue the debate and make sure that the eu issues are integrated into the normal political debate, including by civil society in ireland from now on, and in the rest of europe in the same way. this is part of the reason why we did not have a higher voter turnout: that it is not part of the daily discussions about politics in each and every member state. that has to be done and i hope that there will be a commissioner responsible for both citizenship and communication from now on and hopefully under the new lisbon treaty.
thank you, commissioner, and my thanks to all of you who took part in this very important debate on the outcome of the irish referendum. the debate is closed. written statements (rule 149)

madam president, the developing countries have contributed the least to climate change. but at the same time, they're experiencing the really worst effects. in africa, millions of people need to travel longer and longer distances each day, year after year, to find water, firewood to cook, and food. they are people who depend on the services that natural ecosystems provide, and they are people who have contributed nothing to climate change. in these natural ecosystems, such as the forests of africa, there is three times as much carbon stored as has been released into the atmosphere. it's three times more carbon than is in the atmosphere right now, and it's absorbing 50 percent of the carbon we put into the atmosphere every year. for these reasons, the protection and expansion of forests, based on natural processes and not for commercial purposes, should be a top priority of our policies for both tackling and adapting developed and developing countries to climate change.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. two points: it would be a great mistake to treat shipping and aviation as the same thing. shipping is the most efficient and environmentally friendly mode of transport, while air transport is the relatively worst. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies, and that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. by disproportionately affecting shipping in relation to road transport, the economies of developing countries are in fact disproportionately affected, because developing countries rely mainly on raw materials, agricultural products and industry which mainly use shipping, whereas our economies are mainly service economies. will we convince developing countries of our good intentions by proposing to fund the climate change effort with money that we will get, disproportionately, through shipping and that will burden developing economies more as a percentage of gdp than developed economies? i also wonder, after being informed that the green group yesterday refused to discuss the correlation between the shipping targets and those for land transport, whether running 180 km in your luxury car on a european motorway is more environmentally friendly than transporting food and raw materials for the world economy.
madam president, the main challenge facing us fifty days before the copenhagen summit is whether we can reach an agreement on the responsibility of developing countries for global financing of climate change mitigation policies. europe has made important strides in identifying sources of funding and ways of organising it, but now is the time to press the rest of the developed countries to take their share of the responsibility, bearing in mind that supporting the effort of developing countries to tackle climate change is also an opportunity to combat global inequalities and to reduce the gap between developed and developing countries. there is one more thing that we must not forget, especially those of us in the european union. we are facing the creation of a new class of refugees, climate refugees, who are not protected today by any international treaty and therefore have no rights. the european union is committed to the principle of non-refoulement and to the principle of non-refoulement.

member of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, although the number of women participating in decision-making has increased over the last few years, political and economic power is still firmly in the hands of men. stereotypes associated with gender roles and the difficulty of balancing work and family responsibilities, alongside other overt or covert discriminatory practices, constitute a barrier to women's access to positions of responsibility and leadership. over the last decade, women have become more involved in political decision-making in most european union countries. the pace of these changes is slow and the overall proportion of women in decision-making positions remains unfortunately low. women are very underrepresented in decision-making positions in the economic sphere. in the private sector, nine out of ten members of the management or administrative boards of europe's best companies are men. only 3% of these companies have a female ceo. the commission is keen to promote the better representation of women in senior management positions and supports member states' activities in this regard through the collection, analysis and dissemination of comparable data in this area, the promotion of networks between stakeholders, the exchange of experience and best practices at european level. the european network for women in leadership, launched by the commission in june 2008, highlighted the importance of measures to promote mentoring, develop networks for women in leadership, raise the profile of women in key positions, and encourage women to seek leadership. the commission will organise information activities and exchanges of best practices during 2010. the commission notes with satisfaction that many member states have launched initiatives to promote women's access to management positions, in particular through awareness-raising in the private sector, training programmes for women interested in a career at the highest level, the development of charters or brands for businesses that promote gender equality, the introduction of codes of good practice in businesses and actions to promote reconciliation of family and professional life and to combat gender bias. the issue of quotas for women on the boards of large companies is being discussed. in this respect, i think it is very necessary to study the experience of vice-president of the commission. − the commission pays great attention to all corruption cases all over europe, but i must say that the number of current matters directly and indirectly involving siemens in which eu funds are involved is quite limited. the following is an overview of four of the matters concerned. a case currently investigated by olaf and closed in the last quarter of 2003, involving external aid, is in judicial follow-up in germany. olaf is following closely the judicial proceedings in that country. a second olaf case relating to projects funded by the eib is currently being investigated. it involves public procurement procedures. thirdly, another matter relating to projects funded by the european investment bank and involving public procurement procedures is currently being evaluated by olaf, in order to determine whether there are sufficiently serious suspicions of fraud or irregularity detrimental to the financial interests of the eu. based on the outcome of this evaluation, olaf will decide if a case needs to be opened in this matter. finally, fourthly, the spanish supreme court issued a ruling on 4 november 2008, in a case involving structural funds and in which siemens was originally implicated. it was investigated by the national authorities in the mid-1990s, and the resulting judicial proceedings have been closely followed by uclaf and subsequently olaf. this ruling, inter alia, sentenced several persons to terms of imprisonment, and imposed fines, for forgery. however, it should also be noted that in this instance siemens was acquitted in the case in the first ruling of the criminal court of madrid issued on 22 june 2006. as with all these kinds of cases, the general point is that olaf is not a law enforcement agency. olaf cooperates closely with member states; the latter have an obligation to inform olaf, and olaf naturally follows up and gives serious attention to all the cases where eu funds are somehow involved or have been under investigation. this is the general picture. olaf also participates closely in international cooperation with all other international institutions in combating misuse of money made available for humanitarian aid and other projects. in relation to possible specific case elements, if the honourable member is in possession of material which could be relevant to such matters, the commission would encourage him to transmit it to olaf, which will assess it and take appropriate decisions on it in accordance with its mandate.

mr president, the statements by the council and the commission on the forthcoming european council summit signal the intensification of the anti-people policy of the european union and the governments in its member states with a new, fierce attack against the working class and the popular strata. the primary objective of the european union and its member state governments, whether centre-right or centre-left, is to ensure the continued profitability of the monopolies by shifting the burden of the capitalist economic crisis onto the workers and the popular strata throughout the european union. while the european union has given hundreds of billions of euros to the monopoly giants, without excluding a new round of financing, this new phase is characterized by the more rapid promotion of the capitalist restructuring planned under the lisbon strategy. the anti-union attack is focused on the abolition of the eight-hour day, collective agreements and the widespread implementation of welfare, flexible and low-paid temporary employment by strengthening the institution of local employment contracts and stages. the public systems of social security, health, welfare and education are being brought into the criminal's lap by sweeping changes to the detriment of workers, further opening the way for the penetration of monopoly business groups into these gold-bearing sectors for capital. in the same period, mass redundancies, over five and a half million in the last year, employers' terrorism and the intensification of work have created medieval conditions in the workplace. the most striking example is the continuing crime against the workers of france telecom, where 25 workers have died as a result of unacceptable working conditions and the intensification of work.
mr president, my speech will focus on three points. the first point is the economic crisis. the crisis has affected all economic systems, both strong and weak, indiscriminately. the reading of the ten-year experience of emu in the light of the economic crisis demonstrates the need for greater regulatory europeanisation of economic policy. it also demonstrates the need to europeanise financial risk. trust is good, control is even better. the second point is climate change. the european union is rightly at the forefront of global environmental diplomacy. i fully agree with what president barroso, the president of the commission, said about the absence of a plan b in the run-up to the copenhagen conference. the ecological debt is borne by everyone without exception. i support the idea of a global climate bank, which would draw on resources from the emissions market to finance the efforts of developing countries to create environmentally friendly development models. the third point is immigration. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but there are also external aspects of migration. we must move quickly towards establishing effective external relations with third countries because the pressure on the countries - particularly those in the south of europe - is great. i want to make it clear that turkey's attitude is provocative. i am glad to hear the swedish presidency point this out. the european union's message to turkey, and to other countries, must be clear, precise and effective. this framework is part of the community acquis and must be respected by all.

in writing. i supported the creation of a 'milk fund' to help the sector in crisis, in particular to help farmers with the most urgent problems. i voted in particular for the amendment calling for €20 million more than the european commission had announced, bringing it up to €300 million. it is an amendment to the single common market organisation (cmo) which gives the european commission the power to take emergency measures on its own initiative in the event of serious disturbances on the milk market, as has been the case for several months. this envelope is obviously not sufficient to get all producers out of the rut in which they find themselves; but with the current budgetary margins, unfortunately he would not have been responsible for asking for more. and by asking for more, we also risked getting nothing at all. i want to remind you that these are only milestones that are being set. the debate on the regulatory architecture to be put in place after 2013 is ongoing and, on this issue, the european parliament will continue its efforts to achieve an effective and responsible framework for agricultural markets. the lisbon treaty will provide us with new weapons in this area.

madam president, i consider the problem of combating neurodegenerative diseases, and particularly alzheimer's, to be very serious. it is therefore very useful to have and to achieve coordination of the actions of the countries of the european union in order to tackle the causes, to tackle the problem of prevention, treatment and the consequences of such diseases for patients, the wider public and the public health of the citizens of the european union. i believe that the priority and the main direction should be in the field of prevention and not in the field of medication. the member states should also be encouraged to set up monitoring centres for patients and their carers and to ensure equal scientific participation of states in this european union research effort. finally, the data generated by the database, in consultation with the council and the member states, must be publicly and nationally owned within national systems and the conclusions communicated in public global forums. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them.
madam president, the great challenge of humanity in the face of climate change is how do we stop accumulating carbon in the atmosphere and start to re-store it in the earth. given that it is unlikely that we will stop emitting carbon in the next 50 years, increasing carbon storage on earth is our only hope and should be a priority of response policies. to achieve this, we need to increase the amount of vegetation. in this context, china has planted 540 million acres of trees, with impressive effects on drinking water availability and agricultural productivity. similar forestry programs in rwanda have resulted in such an increase in the flow of rivers to the capital that it can now be powered exclusively by a hydroelectric plant. to this end, the us house of representatives has passed legislation under which the us will pay $5 billion a year to combat deforestation. for these reasons, the council's position is unacceptable, not only because it has not committed itself to a specific amount of financing for developing countries, but also because it makes no mention of financing for the conservation of the world's forests, at the same time as there is no uniform policy and no coordination at european union level for the protection of the threatened forest ecosystems of the european south. i hope, however, that the european union negotiators in copenhagen will give strong support to the global efforts to stop deforestation and increase forestry.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. - the mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, on 21 april 2009 the european ombudsman presented to the president-in-office of the european parliament, mr hans-gert poettering, the annual report on his activities for the year 2008. on 14 september of the same year, mr nikiforos diamanturos presented the contents of the same report to the committee on petitions, which had already instructed the speaker to draw up a recommendation which was unanimously adopted by the members of the committee on petitions on 1 october 2009. the written report shall consist of two forms. a short, outdated summary and an extended version of it, detailing the ombudsman's achievements, statistics and interpretative approach, with a view to improving the process and best practice. the data and findings - in both forms - are presented in a reader-friendly way and are supported by illustrative examples that facilitate understanding, evaluation and exploitation of the text. compared with earlier approaches, this new way of presenting statistical data and evaluating conclusions has made the report more comprehensible, but above all more useful, because it goes beyond the limits of a survey and proceeds to policy issues and ways of improvement. an unprecedented number of investigations were completed during 2008. the majority of cases concerned the european commission and far fewer the administration of the european parliament. the most common form of maladministration concerned a lack of transparency. one third of the cases were settled amicably in favour of the party concerned. there were fewer cases where the ombudsman had to make critical remarks to the administrations involved. the commission has also been asked to provide a detailed reply to a number of questions, and in one case a special report to parliament was used to secure a resolution in favour of the injured party. in 2008, the ombudsman concluded an inquiry on his own initiative into delays in payment by the commission, succeeding in taking measures to limit the phenomenon and announcing a review of the matter. the ombudsman's main priority has been to ensure respect for citizens' rights with a view to strengthening the trust of european citizens in its institutions. this was the purpose of his choice to improve the quality of information given to citizens about their rights through the european ombudsman's network. in parallel with the usual practices for resolving the issues imposed by his institutional role, the ombudsman strengthened his contacts with the members and officials of the european institutions, thus promoting a culture of service-mindedness in the union's administrative services, which certainly contributes to the overall objective of mutual respect between the european citizen and the european institutions. as a direct result of this campaign, the number of referrals increased in 2008, indicating that more european citizens were informed and decided to make use of the ombudsman's institution to report on issues relating to the good administration and functioning of the administrative services of the european union, but not only. the ombudsman's website was regularly updated throughout 2008 to develop into a modern, dynamic and interactive service. in concluding the first phase of this oral recommendation for the european ombudsman's annual report, we expect that constructive activity with the european parliament will continue in the same way, that the institution's activity as a model of good administration for national administrations will be promoted and that the institution will remain a channel of communication between the european institutions and european citizens.
on behalf of the efd group. mr president, i should like to start by saying that we support the ombudsman's report and mrs paliadeli's recommendation, which are both very good and for which we congratulate them. it is a lesson of history that in certain periods of time, but particularly in times of economic crisis, there is a marked disillusionment of citizens with political systems for the administration of justice, while at the same time the increase in the influence of the state at the economic and social level creates an increase in conflicts between citizens and state bodies. the point is that the political system - always, of course, but especially in times like these - must create obstacles to restoring citizens' trust in the administration, the state and the communities. i would venture to say that the counterbalance to the increase in corruption and mismanagement is the increase in the moralization of institutions and the impartiality of the audit bodies. in line with the above considerations, it is understandable to consider that the institution of the european ombudsman is very important in restoring the confidence of european citizens in the institutions and bodies of the european union, and we therefore support any effort to strengthen the institution, to extend its powers and to improve its public image. i therefore ask that we all make every effort to support the work of the european ombudsman and to adopt a citizen-centred approach in all aspects of his work. i'm not sure i'm ready to go.
mr president, i support and vote for mrs palia delli's report and i would like to congratulate mr diamantouros on the work he has done. i would like to stress the fact that a large number of european citizens are often confronted with what we euphemistically call maladministration, which deprives them - after all - of basic rights. in my view, these phenomena are not due to bureaucracy or negligence, but are often due to wrong and unfair policies pursued by the institutions of the european union. the european ombudsman is therefore a means for european citizens to enforce their rights. it is therefore important, mr president, that the european parliament, the only directly elected and representative body of the european union, should give political support to the work of the european ombudsman in order to limit, as far as possible, such phenomena. i would like to say that the report shows that the european ombudsman, and the work of the ombudsman in particular, can, with such support from the european parliament, carry out his work more effectively in the future. in conclusion, i think it is necessary to make an effort to inform european citizens more fully about the role and the possibilities of the european ombudsman, so that they can quickly turn to him when their basic rights are being violated.
mr president, i thank mrs paliadeli for her report. the role of the european ombudsman is indeed particularly crucial, especially at a time when, with the lisbon treaty, we are trying to create a europe closer to the citizen. the role of the european ombudsman is therefore crucial and decisive. i think mr diamantoro has been effective, consistent, worked to promote transparency and address issues related to the lack of it. in addition, it has worked to ensure effective means of redress, both within the european union and within the member states themselves. in this way, a general spirit of service to the citizens by the european institutions is emerging, and of course the citizen - through this whole process and with the effort we must continue to make - feels more confident towards all of us, towards the institutions of the european union. i therefore believe that mr diamantoro has been successful in his work and i think that today's debate and the report presented are decisive for the election of the new european ombudsman for the next term.
european ombudsman. − mr president, let me begin by thanking very warmly all the members of parliament who have made very constructive and very positive remarks about the work of the ombudsman. i deeply appreciate this. i also thank those that have made constructive critical remarks about the work of the ombudsman. that is exactly what i am here for: to learn from your suggestions and your criticisms so as to be able to carry forward the work of the ombudsman, the better to serve citizens in the future. let me say very briefly that the major concerns that i understand you to have expressed have to do with the need for greater transparency and, if re-elected, i will certainly try to redouble my efforts to promote transparency and good administration, for which i feel primarily responsible in the union. the lisbon treaty opens up all sorts of new possibilities, and i certainly intend to make the maximum use of the opportunities allowed me by the treaty, again in order to serve citizens better, cooperating at all times with parliament’s committee on petitions and with this august body. having said that, i would also briefly like to thank commissioner wallström for her work, for her comments and warm words of support, and to repeat and confirm the fact that, as the commission comprises 66% of the union’s entire civil service, it is inevitable that most complaints will be brought against the commission. that is certainly the case. let me address the issues which were brought forward by mr boştinaru and mr czarnecki on the issue of greater cooperation with national ombudsmen, particularly also beyond the eu borders. there are two points here. i certainly have sustained contacts with all the ombudsmen from the candidate countries, who are beyond the eu borders in that sense. beyond that i have very close cooperation with the commissioner for human rights of the council of europe, who has been designated by his institution as the point of contact for all the ombudsmen within the council of europe. my own feeling is that, were i to go beyond that point, i would be entering into the area of international relations contacts, which in fact are the pre-eminent domain of the commission, so i tried to strike a balance in that area. however, i am acutely aware of the need for greater cooperation in all areas and i try to achieve it. on the amount of time that it takes to resolve cases, touched on by mrs göncz, let me just say that we have now been able to reduce the time very significantly, and on average more than 50% – about 55% – of all cases are now closed in less than a year or in about 12 months. if you bear in mind that we need to work in 23 languages, which requires a great deal of translation, i think this is not an excessive time. i am giving you an average, because the simple cases are usually resolved in about three or four months; i just wanted to clarify that point. let me in her absence thank and applaud mrs harkin’s position of support for my position concerning greater transparency even in what are perhaps difficult situations, if i may put it thus. in response to mr vlasák’s comments about the ombudsman, i take these comments very much to heart. but let me point out that the ombudsman should not be judged only in terms of the number of complaints or inquiries that he handles. we handle more than 11 000 requests for information in addition to complaints each year. i travel extensively throughout the member states at all times. during my mandate i have made more than 350 trips throughout the union, and also have reached out to all sorts of constituencies and spoken in large numbers of areas. so the resources that are being expended by the ombudsman to serve citizens go far beyond merely the number of complaints that are handled, and i would like this august body to bear that in mind. i am sure that most of you do know this, but i want to just make that clarification. lastly allow me, since this is the last time that i will address this body during this current mandate, to express from this position my sincere thanks to all the interpreters for the work they have done for me for the past five years.

in writing. first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his excellent work in a very short time. the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon is an important step towards the unification of the european union, the strengthening of its institutions and the deepening of our european identity. the treaty of lisbon also introduces important changes to the procedure for adopting the community budget. in view of the specificities of the budgetary procedure, we must, in the course of the forthcoming meetings with the council, secure the adoption of transitional guidelines until the legal texts provided for in the new treaty have been adopted. these transitional provisions are intended to ensure equal treatment of the institutions - and in particular of parliament - in accordance with the new powers which it will acquire under the new treaty. however, apart from the adoption of the transitional provisions, our priority must be to propose as soon as possible and then to adopt the regulation which will include both the union's multiannual financial framework and the financial regulation. the european commission's proposals for these two texts should be presented as a package to be negotiated jointly with the council.
mr president, i would like to explain my vote on the right of citizens of the western balkans to obtain a visa. the left's constant position is that it is the right of all people to move and travel freely, therefore every citizen should have the right to move and enter the european union. while i therefore agree with the lifting of restrictions and visa requirements for the inhabitants of the western balkans, i voted against the vote on the subject on the grounds that the way in which it puts the issue and refers to kosovo is essentially a de facto confirmation of its unilateral recognition as an independent state. this, in my view, violates the un charter and security council resolution 1244 of 1999. it is a practice that is drifting in a direction that does not promote the peaceful settlement of disputes, security and stability in the region.

member of the commission. − mr president, i think that i can join all those who have said that this a nice piece of collaboration in order to get a nice piece of legislation. no piece of legislation is perfect; this one is not perfect, either, and you know how much time we need in order to set up new legislation – so that by the time we arrive at an agreement, the world has already advanced so much that we should start from the beginning again. that is exactly the reason why we said that the protection of the individual rights on net neutrality is the first step; another step which needs to follow is how the copyright has to be adapted to the online world. because we cannot wait until all these rules are implemented into national law, i have, in the name of the commission, said that the
