to see if we need to take other, supplementary measures or if we have to push to implement the existing measures which we are going to vote on tomorrow in the action at the level of the member states. two concrete answers to two concrete questions: first, the declaration on article 19 relating to harmonisation procedures. exactly like parliament, i regret that 16 member sates have made a declaration which calls into question the scope of the commission’s powers, agreed between the parliament and the council under the amended article 19, in particular the commission’s power in relation to the regulatory obligations that might be imposed by national regulatory authorities (nras). so, because there were those 16 declarations, the commission has also made a declaration pointing out that, while it may not take decisions under the article that refer to specific notifications by nras under article 7a, it may take decisions in relation to general regulatory approaches relating to the imposition, maintenance, amendment or withdrawal of such obligations. parliament is right, an agreement has been made and we should not come back to such an agreement by the back door. second, the question of cookies. now the commission was, like mr harbour, surprised that certain member states appeared to call the agreed text on cookies into question. let me be very clear: we agreed with parliament, and we believe that the final text is unambiguous. first, there must be clear and comprehensive information to users on the basis of which second users must give their consent. that is that and that should be applied now in the member states. i do not appreciate it that after everything has been agreed, some like to manoeuvre in order not to keep 100% to the agreements pacta sunt servanda in politics. that is the way i see things. so i am very proud of the european institutions. i think that they have managed to make a good piece of legislation. they have also managed to keep this equilibrium between the interests of the operators, the economical part of the rules, and the interests of the users, the citizens’ rights, and this equilibrium i think is what europe is about: europe is about economy and society. here in this text we have managed to bring both together. congratulations to all those who have contributed to make this possible.

in writing. the european parliament's centre-left and centre-right political forces are voting in favour of the telecommunications and internet legislative package on the grounds of competition and security, i.e. safeguarding the profits of monopolies and restricting the freedoms and rights of workers. the same political forces, while using demagogic slogans to defend the rights of users and free access to the internet against the arbitrariness of monopoly business groups, supported the reactionary proposals of the commission, helping to promote the interests of capital. the european parliament resolution promotes capitalist restructuring in order to make companies gigantic in the perspective of developing the 'green economy', in order to dominate at european and global level by multiplying their profits at the expense of workers and users of their services. monopolies are legitimized to monitor and restrict users' access to the internet. at the same time, their profits are ensured by the harmonisation of the radio spectrum and the 'functional separation' of fixed telephony and internet services from the necessary infrastructure. we oppose the council-european parliament proposal and stand in solidarity with workers and users of electronic communications who continue to assert their rights and freedoms against the reactionary policy of the eu, the parties of capital.

member of the commission. − my answer to the last question is no, this is a completely different thing. it is about the origin of the product. we are talking now about health claims. when a producer makes a claim that a product is good for your health, for this or that reason, then it has to be scientifically substantiated that this really is so. as regards the other question, of course efsa examines health claims made at the time of application, on the basis of the scientific evidence given at the time of application. obviously, if a claim is made on the basis of certain scientific evidence and the producer then alters the product, this is clearly a question of fraud, and action will be taken against that producer, because they are not only misleading the public but also defrauding the public.
member of the commission. − the question is about varying consumer rights and is very timely. in all member states, there are institutions and organisations that have the task of promoting knowledge and understanding of consumer rights for their citizens. an overview of these national institutions and organisations is available on the website. i could of course provide you with the website. it is http://ec.europa.eu/consumers/empowerment/cons_networks_en.htm, and it is really very interesting to browse around. the commission promotes awareness of consumer rights through various instruments. we sponsor an eu-wide network of consumer centres that provides consumers with information on their rights when shopping across borders and supports them with seeking redress when something goes wrong. the centres’ websites, leaflets and brochures explain to consumers what their rights are, for example, when shopping online, renting a car or booking holidays in another member states. the commission would therefore recommend that members of the european parliament refer their constituents to the european consumer centre located in their respective country. moreover, the commission conducts information campaigns in member states who newly joined the eu to raise awareness of consumer rights and promote national organisations and institutions where consumers can find further help and assistance. the commission also promotes knowledge of rights through its consumer education initiatives such as the ‘europe diary’ that targets students aged between 15 and 18 and dolceta – a website targeting adults and teachers. last but not least, in october 2008 the commission presented its proposal for a directive on consumer rights, which is currently the subject of negotiations in parliament and council. this directive, if adopted, will reduce the current fragmentation of the consumer acquis and ensure that all consumers in the eu will benefit from a single simple set of consumer rights, and this would make it easier to conduct pan-european education campaigns on consumer rights. the commission is currently carrying out research and fact-finding on information to consumers at the point of sale, and it intends to consult retail experts, consumer organisations and other stakeholders on the ground before proposing concrete measures. i also have some news which is very important for us. we have two new extensions to the dolceta information website. we have added two more areas to it: one is sustainable consumption, and the other is services of general interest, so we are expanding.
member of the commission. − this is very much in the domain of the public authorities in the member states. the commission could ask to see that there are public authorities engaged with this. we have a network of such authorities. we gather information from time to time, but our focus is, on the subsidiarity level, on what we could do at cross-border or pan-european level. otherwise, it is very much divided from country to country. this is part of education, and as we know, education is very much in the hands of member states. regarding complaints, what i do is gather together all the complaints and, within the framework of the consumer market scoreboard, see what level those complaints are at, compare them with consumer satisfaction, and then draw the necessary conclusions as to how consumers are treated. you are absolutely right that we need to compare how many complaints are coming in different domains. that is why we propose – and are working on – a common complaint form in europe, because the information which currently comes to the public authorities in country ‘x’ is not filed in the same way as it is filed in country ‘z’. if we have this common complaint form, it is not so that the commission will become a kind of ombudsman on consumer affairs, but to develop our policy from these complaints and be more relevant to citizens. i believe that this really is an important project for the future. we do really have quite limited rights to intervene in how the member states deal with national complaints and how they respond. we could make a scoreboard and we can set a benchmark, but we cannot substitute the presence of the particular member state. i am very eager to continue in the european commission, together with parliament, to benchmark and measure how consumers are treated and how much member states invest in this policy. i think it is a very good investment, especially now in difficult economic times, to see how consumers are treated, because this member of the commission. − the commission is well aware of this practice by car hire companies and has responded to a number of other parliamentary questions and citizens’ complaints about it. i personally receive many of them. i have said before that such a practice is unacceptable if it results in consumers paying for fuel they have not used without their being clearly informed in advance. the package travel directive would only apply if car rentals are included in a holiday package. however, the package travel directive does not regulate this particular issue. on the other hand, these practices could be contrary to the unfair commercial practices directive and the unfair contract terms directive. first of all, under the unfair contract terms directive standard terms and conditions must not be significantly unbalanced to the detriment of consumers. moreover, contract terms should be drafted in plain and intelligible language. it may be argued that a clause resulting in consumers paying in practice for something they have not consumed is unfair. secondly, the unfair commercial practices directive obliges traders to live up to the standard of professional diligence. traders must be open and up-front about the main characteristics of the service provided. car hire companies who do not inform customers that unconsumed fuel will not be reimbursed may be in breach of this directive. this practice might also be considered as contrary to the standard of professional diligence which this directive requires traders to live up to. however, the honourable member should be aware that it is up to national enforcement authorities to determine whether such practices are unfair and to pursue the offending companies. the commission does not have any enforcement powers; consumers who have purchased cross-border services can, however, complain to the european consumer centre in their home country, which should be able to help them receive redress from traders in other eu countries. but i believe that we need to do everything possible to ensure that there are no loopholes in the legislation and that consumers are not left with a sense of unfairness, hopelessness or helplessness.
member of the commission. − as stated, it is up to the national authorities to enforce the relevant law and, however critical we might be vis-à-vis our european institutions, i think that we need also to bear in mind that we are acting under the principle of subsidiarity and there are certain areas where the commission cannot take direct action. i think it would be very proper if we also stressed the responsibility of the member states; otherwise i think that we have a good story coming from parliament, because the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco) is presently involved in an own-initiative report on consumer protection, for which the rapporteur is ms anna hedh. as agreed during the last imco committee meeting, this own-initiative report will cover both the consumer-market scoreboard and the legislation enforcement which we are talking about, for which the commission had issued a communication on 2 july 2009. we are tackling enforcement, but without the member states it will be very difficult to succeed. but i fully recognise the seriousness of the problem – believe me, i share the same anxiety, receiving lots of complaints and not being able to have one practical line and solve the issue instead of a member state.
member of the commission. − i think that this is one of the most important ideas, which we need to develop in the future. first of all the services directive because, clearly, our economy and our lives depend so much on services, and we cannot just leave companies with a bad reputation to spoil an entire sector of industry. we need to fight against bad practices and we need to always protect consumers. but, est modus in rebus, we really need to find the responsibility of the member state and that of the commission. we have a network of public authorities through which we are making our sweeps around the member states. if we have some kind of a code of conduct, to which mr harbour referred, or we detect a breach of legislation of unfair commercial practices, the centres and the different public authorities might be advised to check all the rental car services in the member states and clean up the market. we did it with airlines, whose websites were offering tickets, we did it with ringtones, we did it with electronic goods. why not step up our efforts and do some kind of enforcement sweep in the car rental industry also?
vice-president of the commission. - mr. deputy, i have to go. that question gives me the opportunity to tell you a most gratifying success story. the 'reducing red tape for european business' programme is one of the pillars of the better regulation programme. i first spoke in the spring of 2006 about my aim to reduce the bureaucratic costs for european businesses of european rules by 25% by 2012 and that this requires the commission to make proposals by the end of 2009. the commission has not only put forward the appropriate proposals, but has done more than that. it did so on the basis of a measurement we carried out throughout europe. everyone can tell you that we are saving 25%, if no one knows what the actual costs of bureaucracy in europe are. we therefore organised the largest survey ever carried out to determine the real costs to european businesses of european legislation and its implementation, in terms of documentation, statistics, information, etc. the result was about what we expected. this cost to european companies is €124 billion a year. this represents about 50% of the total expenditure incurred by european enterprises as bureaucratic burdens. we are therefore producing 50% of it here in strasbourg or brussels. i would now like to add expressly to the british press and british members of this house: it would be completely wrong to conclude that the single market is a mere bureaucracy costing eur 124 billion. if we did not have these european rules, we would have 27 different national rules in all these areas, and the burden on european companies - in so far as they are involved in the single market - would be many times greater. i say this very clearly, with a certain sharpness, because i am really tired of hearing in certain media in europe slanderous remarks about the costs of the internal market. nevertheless, we believe that 124 billion is too much and that by better regulation we can achieve the full objectives of our legislation while reducing the costs. the commission has therefore put forward the appropriate proposals, and the result is as follows, i will give you the figures: the measures already adopted by the legislator, which are therefore already in force, reduce the bureaucratic costs for european companies by eur 7 billion a year. the savings potential of the measures proposed by the commission and not yet adopted by the legislator is €31 billion per year. and the commission is working on some additional proposals which will be tabled shortly, which again represent a saving potential of over 2 billion euros, so that we are coming to a total saving potential of over 40 billion a year. this means that the 25% reduction target will be more than met if the measures are actually adopted by the legislator. the programme is complemented by corresponding national programmes in all the member states. i am pleased to tell you that all 27 member states now have similar programmes in place. it will not surprise you to hear that no member state has yet managed to present the record of success that i have been able to do today.
mr president, commissioner, in the commission's action programme of 22 october you mentioned, among other things, the exemption of micro-entities from accounting requirements. in my view, commissioner, there are other more important obstacles of a legislative, administrative and fiscal nature. very few very small enterprises have an activity with a cross-border dimension; the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules. i would like your answer to this specific but specific question.
vice-president of the commission. - mr. deputy, i have to go. you probably know my personal view, which is a little more radical than yours. i personally am very much of the opinion that micro-enterprises which are not active in the internal market are not our business at all - neither the european legislator nor the european commission. but you know, mr president, that you and i are in the minority on this. in every sitting of this house - and especially at question time - i have heard several proposals relating to the conduct of business by small and micro-enterprises. that means you have a lot of convincing to do. however, by introducing the sme specific test i.e. the sme test , the commission has ensured that the impact assessment looks very closely at the consequences for smes and micro-enterprises and, wherever possible, the principle is to exclude micro-enterprises from the rules. let me give you a recent example. the commission has proposed to exempt micro-enterprises from the european accounting rules. this would save these costs by 7 billion euros a year. i am sorry to tell you that the committee responsible for the european parliament has rejected the commission's proposal and i am sorry to tell you that there is organised opposition to this proposal - you can easily imagine who is organising this opposition. the commission nevertheless maintains its position on this proposal. it is a very central part of our policy for small and mediumsized enterprises and for reducing the bureaucratic costs for the micro-enterprises.
vice-president of the commission. - mr. deputy, i have to go. the lisbon treaty is a change in legal terms: it provides for tourism as a third-level community competence, i.e. the community can act in a complementary way to support the activities of the member states, but cannot propose harmonising legislation in this field. in practice, the lisbon treaty does not change anything in the present situation, because what the lisbon treaty now makes legally possible we have already achieved in the form of voluntary cooperation with the member states a few years ago. in other words, the member states agreed a few years ago that the commission should contribute to tourism policy. we have also been able to take a number of concrete decisions together with the member states. the fact that tourism now has its own place in the european treaty will mean that tourism will be given a greater importance in our overall policy for growth and employment. if i have anything to leave as a legacy to the next commission, it is this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is clear that europe is still the most attractive destination for tourists from all over the world. but we are dealing with massive structural changes. we are facing a lot of competition, especially from asia. we must make a great effort to ensure that europe remains the world leader in tourism. if you ask me what i think is the first thing that is required, i would say: all tourism operators - the regions, the member states and the community institutions - must do more to make europe a better known tourism brand, a quality mark, in the world. we have taken the first steps in this direction and i believe that more is possible. i hope that in the next few years, on the basis of the clear political signal given by the lisbon treaty with regard to tourism policy, we will see greater use made here in parliament, in the council and in the commission of the opportunities for promoting and developing european tourism. i am firmly convinced that this is an area in which we can make a significant contribution to growth and, above all, to jobs in regions of europe where there is virtually no alternative, where tourism alone provides really good jobs.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the global economic crisis has also had a negative impact on tourism. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents at work is a good one, but it is not enough to say that the directive is a good one. secondly, what initiatives will the commission take to boost europe's internal tourism? and thirdly, what measures will you take to attract tourists from other countries outside the european union?
vice-president of the commission. - mr. deputy, i have to go. the first question is, you can always try. during the austrian presidency, several attempts were made to coordinate the start of the holidays, but without any success. i would urge that we make another attempt, but i would caution against expecting too much. the idea that certain tourist areas can coordinate their holiday seasons in europe so that these tourist areas are occupied all year round is an illusion and would not have my support. all people have the right to take holidays in the season that suits them best and is also of the greatest recreational value. but you are absolutely right in saying that neighbouring countries should agree that the holidays do not always and everywhere have to start on the same day. these are things which i believe are possible and which should be seriously addressed not only in the context of tourism policy but also in the context of environmental and transport policy. as regards the second question, we have studied the impact of the economic crisis on tourism in depth, and we found in a survey of over 50 000 people across europe that people are still going on holiday - so they are not giving up their holiday - but the trips are getting shorter, the money is being spent less, and the holidays are being spent more often in their own country. but the same standard is required, i.e. those who can offer a particular price-performance ratio will now benefit. the commission's action in this field can only be complementary to that of the member states. we do not have a per se autonomous european tourism policy, and it would be too much to list the many initiatives which have been taken to promote europe's attractiveness as a tourist destination throughout the world and within europe. i will mention just one example because it has been particularly successful recently. we launched the 'outstanding european destinations' competition three years ago. here tourism regions in europe are asked to present their achievements under certain themes, and here, in three years of experience, it has been shown that in almost all aspects we have excellent tourism opportunities throughout europe. the key is to make these tourist offers known. this is why we have created a web portal that makes it possible to access all tourism-related information from all european member states with a single click.
member of the commission. − mr president, mr mep, as you know, the primary responsibility for developing and implementing policy measures to respond to the crisis and, more specifically, to its impact on employment lies with the member states. despite this, the commission has taken a number of initiatives since the crisis erupted last autumn to mitigate the impact of the financial and economic crisis on the labour market. the european economic recovery plan presented by the commission in november 2008 and endorsed by the european council addresses pressing issues and, in addition, calls for investments that should result in long-term benefits for the union. the recovery plan emphasises the importance of implementing integrated policies, summarised under the term 'flexicurity', in order to protect european citizens from the worst effects of the crisis. in this context, it emphasises the strengthening of activation regimes, retraining and upskilling, better alignment of skills supply with labour market needs and the need to support the most vulnerable. the aim is to protect employment and long-term employment rather than specific jobs. experience shows that the fundamental objectives of flexicurity i.e. the ability to adapt to change and to facilitate transitions between jobs are of the greatest importance in times of economic downturn and increasing labour market instability. the integrated approach provides a coherent policy framework to coordinate efforts to address the employment and social impact of the crisis, and can help to strike a balance between short-term measures to address immediate needs, such as temporary reductions in working hours, and long-term reforms, such as through upskilling and active labour market policies. the recovery plan was followed by an employment summit in may this year. on that occasion, the key stakeholders expressed a common commitment to mitigate the impact of the economic crisis on employment in the european union. following the employment summit, the commission adopted on 3 june a communication entitled 'a common commitment to employment' which sets out three key priorities for action: maintaining employment, creating jobs and promoting mobility, upskilling and matching the skills supply to labour market needs and improving access to the labour market. these three key priorities were endorsed by the european council in june. the commission is convinced that cooperation with and between the social partners is very important, especially in times of crisis and in the event of restructuring. the social partners have a crucial role to play in the design and implementation of economic recovery measures. at national level, this role is derived from the tradition and practice of involving the social partners in the definition and implementation of labour market policies. consultations are ongoing at european union level on the proposed policies, in particular in the framework of the tripartite social summit. cooperation with the european union's social partners has intensified this year in connection with preparations for the abovementioned employment summit and the communication of 3 june.
we are pleased that you too, mr president, mr commissioner, as your speech showed, consider the active involvement of the social partners to be extremely important for the democratic character and effectiveness of the social dialogue, especially at this crucial moment of the economic crisis, when we are faced with a decline in employment, unemployment, precarious work and so much more. however, given the purely formal nature of this participation so far, we would like to know whether the commission intends to take specific institutional measures to ensure that there is a real involvement at both european and national level, with specific directives from the european union.
member of the commission. - ladies and gentlemen, i must say that article 138 of the ec treaty sets out clearly the position of the social partners and their role in the discussion of all important social issues. the commission makes full use of this framework, which is why there have also been agreements between the social partners in some areas that have been transposed or are in the process of being transposed into european directives. as regards institutional or legislative changes, you are well aware that the european commission proposed and, in the context of this debate, adopted an amendment to the european works council directive, which strengthens the position of the european social partners, particularly in the negotiations on transnational restructuring. you know as well as i do that the european commission supports the use of all these instruments to intervene effectively in the labour market. i would also like to emphasise that the european commission is also very active in international organisations, i.e. it actively promotes and supports the application of the ilo's fundamental agreements and conventions both at global level and, of course, at european level. in any event, during the mandate of this commission, there has been a shift in the framework of cooperation with the social partners, and i am sure that this path will continue, because, as i have already stated, the debate with the social partners is part of the ec treaty, it is a specific feature of our european law and, in my view, it is undeniable progress.

vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am speaking on behalf of mrs ashton. as regards the euro-mediterranean free trade area, its establishment by 2010 was a realistic objective and we will have made considerable progress towards this goal by next year, although it is clear that much remains to be done to fully realise the potential for economic integration in the euro-mediterranean area. progress has been made particularly in the north-south dimension. the european union has concluded bilateral association agreements with all its mediterranean partners, with the exception of syria, which mainly concern the exchange of goods. however, further bilateral negotiations are under way to further promote trade in agricultural products, services and freedom of establishment, and to establish a binding dispute settlement mechanism. some of these negotiations have already been concluded and others are expected to be concluded by 2010. as regards the new negotiations between the european union and the mediterranean partners and the role of the european parliament, i can say that, again in the north-south dimension, at the euro-mediterranean trade ministers' conference on 9 december we expect to reach agreement on a euro-mediterranean trade roadmap beyond 2010, reflecting the objective of gradually transforming the current euromed association agreements into comprehensive and far-reaching free trade agreements. it is not a question of negotiating new association agreements, but rather of developing and strengthening existing ones to address issues such as trade facilitation, technical barriers and sanitary and phytosanitary issues, as well as public procurement, competition, intellectual property rights, trade and aspects relating to sustainable development and transparency. bilateral negotiations will be tailored to the situation of each partner in the southern mediterranean. morocco could be the first mediterranean country with which negotiations will be opened next year. it is clear that, with the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon, this parliament will have a strengthened role in trade. the commission is ready to work closely with you in the future negotiations i have just mentioned. the euro-mediterranean free trade area also has a south-south dimension. the mediterranean partners are creating a network of free trade agreements between themselves and the agadir agreement, in force since 2007, is also open to other mediterranean countries. israel and turkey have also concluded free trade agreements with their mediterranean partners, and other agreements are under negotiation. it is too early to make a full assessment of the current agadir agreement. trade between the four partners increased, albeit less than initially expected. this may be due to a number of reasons, such as the existence of non-tariff barriers, the lack of complementarity between the different markets, the lack of a regional market capable of attracting investors and, last but not least, the fact that companies are not sufficiently aware of the possibilities offered by such agreements. the euro-mediterranean roadmap on trade after 2010 should help to overcome this problem. it also includes a number of concrete proposals for the short term. one of them is the euro-mediterranean trade and investment facility. the establishment of this mechanism will provide free, up-to-date, comprehensive and easily accessible information on trade, investment conditions and regulation in the euro-mediterranean region and aims to help businesses, in particular small and medium-sized enterprises, to operate on euro-mediterranean markets. as regards sustainability, as with all our free trade areas, the commission carried out a sustainability impact assessment, which was completed in december 2007 and is used for both current and future negotiations. in particular, on trade-related issues, the stability pact stressed the importance of long transitional periods which had been provided for in the association agreements for the elimination of industrial tariffs by the southern mediterranean countries and the need to provide for such transitional periods in the negotiations on agricultural projects currently under way. the need to take account of the level of development of the southern mediterranean partners in the current talks on services and establishment also emerged. in addition, in many of these countries the commission is supporting tax reform programmes that can help to reduce the negative effects of the decline in tariff revenues reported in the sia. as regards the agreement with syria, to which i referred earlier, in 2008 the commission launched a review of the draft association agreement in order to determine whether technical amendments and updates are necessary before it can be concluded. a round of talks was enough, in which we agreed with the syrians on the changes to be made to take account of the accession of romania and bulgaria to the european union and the tariff changes introduced in both syria and the union after the suspension of the talks in 2004. the agreement was signed in december 2008. last month we announced that the union is now ready for signature. syria has delayed the conclusion of the agreement to analyse its economic implications. as regards libya, negotiations were opened in november 2008 in brussels to conclude a framework agreement centred on an ambitious free trade agreement on goods, services and freedom of establishment, as well as regulatory cooperation. the conclusion of this agreement with libya will be the final piece of the puzzle. the union will have concluded free trade agreements with all its mediterranean neighbours by then, even though libya is not part of the union's neighbourhood policy. with the conclusion of this trade agreement, union exporters will find new export opportunities and a more favourable regulatory environment in libya, in particular in the services and oil and natural gas markets. of course, the negotiations with tripoli are still in their early stages and will still require some time to conclude. in this process, the commission will pay particular attention to the development of capacity building within the libyan administration on trade and related issues. a sustainability impact assessment is also being finalised for libya.

mr president, it is important that the treaty of lisbon has finally come into force. the european union has been unable to find the institutional means to function effectively after the enlargement of the union to include 12 new member states. but today we are in a new reality. there is the president of the council - and we are all assured that he is the person who will seek consensus and agreement - and there is the high representative for foreign affairs. it is of no consequence to comment in advance whether they are suitable or not in relation to those who were advertised before or in relation to those who were supposed to have expertise in these matters. the question is how the treaty is to be applied. the commission's cooperation with the european parliament is also of great importance here. the role of the parliament is being strengthened, but in order to strengthen it in substance and to make it clear to the european citizens, it is necessary that the european commission urgently raise the issues, since it has the initiative in the european council. the council is supposed to work more quickly, since it will no longer function as before with the presidencies of the member states and governments will not be given the opportunity to use the councils to claim that what is good is done by governments and what is bad and annoying is done by brussels. the council of ministers has already taken a decision on the matter, and i am sure that the commission will take a decision on it in the near future. we have been told about other subjects in general. it is true that enlargement to the western balkans and turkey, on the basis of the european commission's proposals, is faced with a number of doubts and a number of challenges, despite the objective of these countries' accession to the european union.
in writing. the kke is opposed to and votes against the amendments to the rules of procedure of the european parliament to bring them into line with the provisions of the treaty of lisbon. the amendments maintain and reinforce the reactionary and anti-democratic character of the european parliament's rules of procedure, which form a suffocating framework for any opposing voice opposing the domination of the political representatives of capital. it is a lie that the lisbon treaty gives the eu a 'more democratic dimension' because it allegedly upgrades the role of the european parliament. the european parliament is a component of the reactionary structure of the eu. it has proved its commitment to the reactionary policy of the eu, its support for the interests of the monopolies, its role as an instrument of democratic legitimization of the eu's anti-people policy. the european parliament does not represent the people but the interests of capital. it is in the interest of the peoples to resist, to break with the anti-people policy of the eu and the european parliament which supports it, to overthrow the european union's structure.
in writing. i voted against, firstly because amendment no 13 was adopted, which considers nuclear energy to be an important component in reducing carbon dioxide emissions, and secondly because amendment no 3 by my group, which called for developed countries to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions below 1990 levels by 80 and 95% by 2050, was rejected. there are many positive elements in the resolution, such as the eu committing €30 billion per year by 2020 to meet the needs of developing countries in the area of climate change mitigation and adaptation. but i think that turning to nuclear energy as an antidote to global warming is not only not the solution to combating climate change, it is a dangerous option. the trio of developed - developing - underdeveloped countries and governments - grassroots movements - people will collide in copenhagen as climate change greatly undermines efforts to reduce poverty and hunger in the world. the copenhagen conference is a real challenge to which we must respond and not let the industrial and nuclear lobbies prove to be the big winners.
in writing. the growing risks to the environment and health, and especially the dangerous climate change and global warming, are the result of industrial development guided by capitalist profit, the commercialization of land, air, energy and water. the real solution to these problems cannot be found in the power of capital, i.e. in the power of those who are responsible for creating them. the road to the copenhagen conference is being blocked by the sharpening of inter-imperialist contradictions. with proposals for a 'sustainable, green economy' and 'low-carbon' economic growth, the eu is trying to further open the way to investment by eu monopolies and at the same time satisfy the speculative expectations of capital through the 'pollutant market'. in order to plan and implement a development path which will contribute to the balanced relationship between man and nature and to the satisfaction of the needs of the people, capitalist production relations must, in the final analysis, be overthrown. the kke votes against the ep resolution. it proposes the combined satisfaction of the people's needs according to the wealth produced in our country. the political conditions for the realization of the above objective are the socialization of the basic means of production and the central planning of economic life, with popular workers' control, the people's power-people's economy.
in writing. the kke is categorically opposed to the stockholm programme, just as it was opposed to previous programmes to implement the eu's pseudo-area of freedom, security and justice (afsj). the aim, despite the eu's demagogic declarations, is to harmonize or homogenize national legislation in order to implement the eu's anti-people policy in a uniform manner; strengthening existing and creating new mechanisms of prosecution and repression at eu level, under the pretext of striking at terrorism and organised crime. the first priority of the 'stockholm programme' is to intensify the eu's anti-communist hysteria, which is in full swing, culminating in the unhistorical and unacceptable equation of communism with nazism. the eu's nap and its programmes not only do not meet the people's interests, but are instead a set of measures which slaughter individual and social rights and democratic freedoms, intensify authoritarianism and repression against workers, migrants and refugees; they shield the political system and the dominance of monopolies; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
in writing. i voted against the proposal for a number of reasons, and i will mention the main ones in detail. in fact, its basic dimension is the philosophy of 'security' and fear, at the expense of basic rights and freedoms. but security is guaranteed precisely by the protection and respect of these rights in a society of law. it strengthens the perception and the possibility of implementing a fortress europe that treats migrants as potential terrorists and criminals and at best 'accepts' their presence, not as people with equal rights, but according to the needs of the eu labour market. it promotes abhorrent mass deportations, does not strengthen the right of access to asylum, opens the way for the eu to be actively involved in refugee camps outside its borders and to impose lenient agreements with third countries, without regard for the safeguarding of human rights. finally, although much more could be said, the resolution consolidates policies that multiply the various bodies that monitor, collect and share personal data of citizens, violating collective and personal dignity and trampling on the right to freedom of expression. this resolution is addressed to a society which has only enemies and where everyone is a suspect, and that is not the society we want.

acting president of the council. - madam president! - what is it? on 1 july 2009, the commission forwarded its proposal for a council recommendation on smoke-free environments, based on article 152 (4) of the ec treaty. the main purpose of this proposal is to implement article 8 of the who framework convention on tobacco control. article 8 deals with protection against exposure to tobacco smoke. to date, the convention has been ratified by 26 member states and the community. on 8 july 2009, in view of the swedish presidency's work programme and in order to allow the other institutions sufficient time to express their views, the council invited the european parliament, the european economic and social committee and the committee of the regions to deliver their opinions by 26 november, 5 november and 8 october 2009 respectively. the european economic and social committee has already adopted its opinion and the committee of the regions has indicated that it does not intend to deliver one. i understand that the european parliament plans to adopt its opinion by march 2010 at the latest, which i regret. unfortunately, it is too late for the council to have the opportunity to consider it. this is definitely not about disrespect. on the contrary, i think so. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the word 'smoking' in the wording of the directive. the text of the recommendation is currently being discussed in the council and we have made considerable progress so far. i am confident that we will achieve our objective of adopting the recommendation at the council meeting on 1 december 2009. however, the council does not intend to adopt any conclusions on this matter. the council has taken note of the european parliament resolution adopted on 24 october 2007 on the green paper 'towards a smoke-free europe: policy options at eu level'. in the resolution, parliament called on member states to introduce legislation on smoke-free smoking within two years. many eu countries have now adopted such laws, and more are on the way. parliament also considered that the policy on smoking cessation should be complemented by other support measures. the council shares that view. i am pleased to have had the opportunity to present to the council the timetable for the draft recommendation on smoke-free environments and look forward to hearing your views on this matter.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the council's decision to rush its proposal for a smoke-free environment to the member states without allowing parliament sufficient time to elaborate its own position is, in our view, wrong. our reaction, apart from today's question, is the resolution, which we shall vote on, i hope, tomorrow and on which we have reached a good compromise which i think will express the great majority of the house. this resolution contains many new elements. we in the pp are particularly proud of the emphasis placed in this text on the protection of children from secondhand smoke, as, on our proposal and with the agreement of the other groups, many new elements have been introduced. i would like to draw attention to the need for special sensitivity and protection, since children, unlike adults, are not able to consent legally, morally or even psychologically to their exposure to secondhand smoke. parents have this duty of protection, but they need our help, since secondhand smoke in children has not been sufficiently studied and therefore neither parents nor anyone else has the knowledge of what the consequences of long-term exposure of children to tobacco are and what the necessary degree of protection is. the commission's proposal to carry out a pan-european study on the problem, including third-hand smoke, is therefore of particular value. the information that will be generated can offer enormous services. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
the next item is the debate on the oral question to the commission on the ratification and application of the revised ilo conventions by alejandro cercas, on behalf of the s&d group, marian harkin, on behalf of the alde group, jean lambert, on behalf of the verts/ale group, gabriele zimmer and ilda figueiredo, on behalf of the gue/ngl group, (o-0131/2009 - b7-0228/2009).
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to thank my colleagues from the epp group and the other political groups in our european parliament, because we have all wanted to come together on this issue of decent work to table a joint resolution, and the negotiations of the past few days have been extremely positive. since 1919, the international labour organization has been able to secure and develop a system of international labour standards covering a wide range of subjects, including labour, employment, social policy and human rights. we cannot forget that, especially in these times of crisis. this is why it is extremely important that the conventions have been classified by the ilo as up to date following a tripartite process involving employers, workers and governments. that is why we have issued this recommendation to the member states, encouraging them to ratify the conventions which have been classified by the ilo and asking them to contribute actively to their effective implementation for the development and advancement of social europe. we want it within a reasonable time. we will be vigilant about the timing of the application of these conventions, while respecting the principle of subsidiarity. the epp group will be particularly vigilant in the application of these conventions in the member states. the fight against illegal work, the modernisation of social progress, the building of a genuine social europe, which shows the way to the world, is at that price, and we really want to take it forward.
the european union is a community of citizens, and we are all aware of the importance of this. we know all too well that the exercise of these rights is a necessary condition for becoming a member state of the union. we also know that when these rights are violated, the union is obliged to impose sanctions. the foundation of these rights, which is inherent in the creation and functioning of the union, extends to the workplace. the importance which the union attaches to the defence of labour rights is evident not only in the production of internal legislation but also in its external policy. we know very well that when it comes to contracts with third countries, one of the conditions it sets is that basic labour rights are respected. for these reasons, the union could not but be one of the first to support and systematically revise the conventions of the international labour organization, the ilo. recently, after negotiations with workers, employers and governments, the ilo has undertaken yet another revision of its conventions. and while the governments of the member states and the union are among those who have participated in this review, we are faced with a paradox. the paradox is that many member states of the union, while having adopted eu directives which impose labour rights more advanced than those of the ilo conventions, underestimate the importance of the formal issue of ratifying these conventions at national level. the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the community's financial resources are used to the full. in any event, commissioner, the european commission should not hesitate to call more forcefully on the member states to fulfil such obligations in order to increase the credibility of the union by promoting decent work, especially in the current context of the financial crisis.

member of the commission. − madam president, i also deplore that none of the g8 leaders were present at rome except for commission president barroso, and of course it adds to the image that this has been a summit which did not bring that much new. when you look at the final declaration this is, i think, also obvious. on the other hand, i think it is also very important that we managed to keep the food security item on the political agenda and the result of the several summits we have been witnessing in 2009 has certainly been that it is now high on the international agenda and that, whenever world leaders meet, for example latterly in pittsburgh for the g20, there is talk of development cooperation and development policy. so that in itself is a very positive element. i have been in rome and i must say that apart from the final declaration which is, i must agree, a little bit disappointing, there have been very good discussions and also a very good presence, and something can come out of it. for example, there was a whole discussion on the selling of fertile land in developing countries and countries which have no arable land; buying them itself is a very interesting topic to discuss and i think it is also one where we can come to some common understanding. the second thing i would like to say is that, as a couple of members have already said, the cap is of course not ideal. nothing is ideal in this world, but when you look at the effect of the common agricultural policy on the developing world, i think we can claim that it is by far the least harmful system of a big trading block with respect to distorting effects in the developing countries. the wto has acknowledged that most if not all of our subsidies are non-trade-distorting because they are supporting agricultural income, not the prices of agricultural products. i am also a little bit, how should i put it, disappointed that we are blaming ourselves all the time. europe is not ideal either but i think that with the food facility, for example, we took a major step forward. this involves eur 1 billion on a two-year basis; it is not about supporting the delivery of food, but is largely focused on delivering seeds and so on sustaining small agricultural producers in the developing world. i think this really is an innovation. it has also been acknowledged as such by the world bank, for example, which is taking over this mechanism. so we should not blame ourselves all the time. by the way, this facility was an innovation by my predecessor. there is one thing i do not agree with him and that was about a gentleman who in the mean time has disappeared, mr le hyaric. he is not a socialist, my predecessor, he is a communist; i mean you should look at his political group: he is a communist and that probably explains the reasoning he was using. having said this, also in l’aquila we took up our responsibility as a european commission and we pledged usd 4 billion, which is about 20% of the food package and the support package that was agreed in l’aquila. with that we are by far the largest donor that made pledges in l’aquila and we are also going to honour them, we are also going to commit this sum and to disburse this as soon as possible. i will end by saying a last word on the new eu agricultural and food security policy because in the 2010 work programme of the commission there is a plan to present to the council and the parliament a communication on a renewed policy for agriculture and food security for the european union. this document will review current issues affecting agriculture and food security, for example, challenges posed by climate change, enhanced attention to nutrition and food quality, safety nets and social protection policies, impact of biofuels on food production or the use and impact of new technologies and biotechnologies, increased calls for rights-based approaches, large-scale land acquisition etc. the communication will aim firstly at renewing the eu commitment to assist developing countries advancing their agricultural production. this remains crucial notably in view of the increasing demand for food due to a growing global population and changing dietary patterns and the challenges and threats that climate change is posing to sustainable agricultural production. secondly, it will aim at launching reflection on how the eu could best use its experience and know-how to support the emergence of regional policies and strategic frameworks in agriculture and food security. thirdly, it will aim at providing the basis for the whole eu approach to harmonise existing ecms policy frameworks following the commitments set out in the l’aquila agenda for action. fourthly, it will aim at proposing ways on how the eu could contribute to accelerating the process towards the achievements of the mdgs and notably mdg1 in view of the upcoming review of the mdgs in september 2010 in new york. fifthly, it will aim at better positioning the eu vis-à-vis current developments in the global food agricultural governance system, and lastly, at addressing issues that have recently gained prominence in the food security agenda. a public consultation on an issues paper was launched on 16 november and will close in early january. thus we will consult all the stakeholders and then come forward with a formal communication by the european commission.

member of the commission. − madam president, may i remind the honourable members that what i said was not that we cannot impose rules on third countries. i am sorry that, even after a presentation of 10 to 15 minutes, i failed to explain the rules we impose when we import from third countries. i must remind you that at the same time as we are importers of meat from third countries – and at this point we import from brazil only 5% of our requirements for beef in the european union – we are also big exporters to third countries: to russia and elsewhere. at this point our exports to russia amount to eur 1 billion per year – mostly from ireland. we are trying to convince russia that we cannot accept the same rules as applied in russia. there are rules of international trade, and we apply equivalent, but not identical, rules provided that we are satisfied that they are strict enough to protect our consumers. this is what we are doing. the reason why we applied the safety rules in the case of brazil is precisely because our fvo missions have shown us that something was going wrong. we applied very strict rules. i gave you the figures. regarding the last mission to brazil, there were indeed problems, but i challenge you to look at all the reports of our fvo missions to any of the member states and make a comparison. you will also see very serious deficiencies in the member states, which we ask member states and their authorities to put right. that is also what we have asked brazil to do. problems were identified in brazil. in one state, where three holdings showed significant deficiencies, the brazilian authorities took corrective action to de-list all the holdings concerned and to retrain the auditors responsible for their audits. problems were also identified at three other holdings, mainly in relation to delays of notification of animal movements or to the consistency of the data in the database. these problems were recognised to be minor problems by the fvo team. nevertheless, the brazilian authorities made an undertaking to review the database to avoid incorrect data. the fvo missions have this in mind when they go. they know that they will find defects. our obligation is to correct the deficiencies that we find – whether this is in member states or in third countries – because our obligation is to our consumers. i want to assure you that we shall continue sending missions to brazil and to other third countries to make sure that when deficiencies are discovered, they are put right. i must also assure you that we are playing a very fair game for our farmers and producers vis-à-vis third countries. there was a remark about romania, which in the same way as bulgaria and – in the past – many other member states had a problem with classical swine fever. we have heard a lot about romania and bulgaria. i must challenge you to ask your respective governments what assistance we have offered in order to get rid of this problem. i am sure that romania will be able to export meat in the very near future precisely because of the assistance we have offered it and bulgaria to get rid of classical swine fever. in ending i want to assure you that our first responsibility is to our european consumers and that we want a just and fair deal for everybody. you can also be assured that our fvo missions to third countries will be very strict. we shall remain vigilant and if something goes wrong we shall try to put it right. we shall remain vigilant and strict. whether more farms are put on the list or not depends entirely on the brazilian authorities, and on whether they are prepared to spend the money that they have to spend in order to have their farms approved in accordance with our standards and to be able to export. if they do not, then they will not.

in writing. women, because of their unequal position in all class societies, experiencing class and racial oppression, are easily victimized by all forms of violence. physical abuse, rape, trafficking, etc. are forms of violence that demonstrate the racial dimension of the class nature of women's inequality. but violence is a social phenomenon with specific economic, political and social causes. these causes which lie at the root of capitalist relations of production cannot be eliminated as long as these relations exist. the popular movements must demand measures to prevent the phenomenon and to provide relief to the victims, women and children, by seeking more radical changes in a pro-people direction at the social and political level. we are radically opposed to the creation of counselling centres and support bodies for abused women by ngos and private individuals. these should be the sole responsibility of the state. the measures proposed in the resolution not only fail to eliminate the problem, leaving the cause intact, but by seeking to manage it they perpetuate it.
in writing. the authors of the resolution, although they are forced to highlight the problem of hunger and more generally the problems raised at the summit of the food and agriculture organization (fao), nevertheless conceal the root cause of the problem, namely the capitalist system of exploitation and the strategy that the eu is loyal to for the profitability of capital. the multinational food production and distribution companies are uprooting farmers and concentrating land, dealing a heavy blow to poor and middle-class farmers. workers and the working class, even in the advanced capitalist countries, are finding it increasingly difficult to secure healthy and safe food, while food multinationals are raking in huge profits and are now buying up vast tracts of land, primarily in africa, on the assumption that food production must double by 2050. the eu is leading the way in liberalising and privatising all food, water, energy, transport and technology sectors, and is imposing this on third countries through the wto and bilateral agreements. the cap is the backbone of this policy of eliminating small and mediumsized farmers and supporting monopolies and their profits. farmers and workers are fighting this policy, fighting to overthrow this system of exploitation and to ensure sufficient healthy and safe food for the entire population.

member of the commission. − mr president, i will now try to respond to some of the questions raised, but i think this discussion here clearly shows that there is a common understanding of the difficulties that our agricultural sector is facing. first of all, i was a bit astonished to hear that we should have removed the safety net to the agricultural sector. that is certainly not the case. we actually have a safety net in place in situations where the farmers need support. we have a continuous safety net, which is the direct payments. that is certainly in place, and that is accountable for the farmers. then we have the different intervention systems, the private storage, the export refunds systems to enter into effect when we find it necessary to trigger these tools. on simplification, red tape, bureaucracy – i completely agree with you that we should try to ease the situation as much as possible for our farmers, and that is the reason why, in the council meeting on wednesday, i will present to the council some deregulation proposals to be discussed. the council presented 39 different proposals, and we can actually accept quite a number. others are more political, related to the period after 2013, but i am sure that you would find the proposals extremely interesting. those that have raised the gmo issue, i presume you know that i think completely along the same lines, and i think that it is important to find a solution on a technical figure on the presence of non-approved gms. i expect the next commission to present such a proposal. it is true that the european union is the biggest importer of agricultural products, but at the same time the european union is the biggest exporter of agricultural products. this is because of the fact that we have an extremely good brand: european products are considered to be high quality on overseas markets. therefore, the idea that we should now protect our own production would be damaging for our agricultural sector. so we need to go on being visible on the export markets, and i think that for the future – and i hope there will be full support from the european parliament – we need much more money for promoting our high-quality products on the new emerging markets. i think we can do much better, but we need a bit of support. then on the issue of the regulatory framework – i did not hear this exact phrase, but i think that is what is behind the comments on this issue. today it is already possible for farmers to enter into a contract with the industry, with the dairy, for a certain volume at a fixed price. it is already possible for farmers to do this. and you know in the high level group for the dairy sector we are looking into some voluntary ways of improving this system. biodiversity: i completely agree that this is important, and that was exactly the reason why we included biodiversity in the new challenges when we agreed on the health check of the common agricultural policy in november 2008. so this has high priority, and i completely agree with the comments from the honourable member. on the measures: we can actually use all the tools that we have available at the moment. but new initiatives cannot be presented by a caretaker commission. i am sure that you are aware of this problem, and therefore i hope that we will be able to see the new commission in operation as soon as possible. next, i am very happy to see that the european parliament will now have a role as an important player for the future, thanks to the lisbon treaty introducing codecision. this is not only codecision on the pure agricultural issues but also on the budget. so here, you will have a huge influence on the possibilities on the budgetary possibilities for the agricultural sector for the period after 2013. though i might not experience it from this place, i can guarantee that i will keep an eye on you when you take upon yourselves the new responsibilities for the agricultural sector.

in writing. the phenomenon of over-employment in the eu is common and is aimed at providing additional income to agriculture. and because a small farm is more likely to have busy members, it's obvious that a small farm today is disproportionately affected compared to a large one. this is because, apart from the reduction in agricultural income, whatever the sector in which it is specialised, it is also faced with the reduction or elimination of its non-agricultural income, while the security income it receives in the form of direct aid is totally insufficient for a decent living. therefore, regardless of the agricultural sector of specialisation, there should be horizontal targeted crisis aid for small farms.
the agenda includes the debate on the oral question to the commission on the prospects for the doha development agenda after the seventh wto ministerial conference, by vital moreira on behalf of the committee on international trade (o-0126/2009 b7-0232/2009)
member of the commission. − mr president, the seventh wto ministerial conference in geneva was an important opportunity to exchange views with all wto members on their priorities for the organisation. i think it is important to underline that this seventh ministerial conference was not a negotiating conference: it was more a discussion on various topics. our priority list, of course, begins with the doha development agenda. if it can be completed, doha will deliver a good outcome not only for europe, but also for the whole world economy. all of us will suffer if protectionism increases, just as all of us will gain if the economy picks up. doha is the best trade policy instrument available on both those fronts. doha would also deliver the development that so many of the poorest countries in the world are looking for. it would do that through new rules on market access, farm reform and customs facilitation in particular. in all areas of the doha negotiations, the development element for the countries concerned has already been taken into account. but of course we can conclude the doha round only if all wto members are on board. the reality is that the united states has serious concerns and constraints which it is discussing with the large emerging economies. in a few months we will see how these discussions are evolving as the deadline for the g20 to conclude the doha in 2010 comes closer. i think we all agree that, if we want a conclusion of the doha round before the end of 2010, we have to see modalities in place on agricultural and non-agricultural market access (nama) before the end of march 2010. that is the ultimate deadline. meanwhile we will continue to push so that the interests of the european union in areas such as trade and services, and protection of geographical indications, are covered. apart from doha, the ministerial conference addressed some important issues for wto members. our priorities included the need to strengthen the role of the wto in monitoring and analysing protectionism; wto accession – such as for instance the accessions of least-developed countries into the wto, which we certainly think must be promoted; the rising member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to refer to what one of the honourable members said – that the european union should be the honest broker in these negotiations. i do not disagree but i do not want the european union to be the only banker in this round. there is a broad recognition that the european union has played a crucial role in keeping the train on track. the reason for this is that in previous rounds we have been very vulnerable because of our agricultural sector, and this has not been the case in these negotiations. we have put a strong offer on the table and this has meant that we are now in a very favourable situation. but i have to say as well – not only here, but i have said it elsewhere, including in the negotiations in geneva – that the european union is not going to give any further concessions in agriculture. we have stretched to the ultimate limit of what we can do and this actually has been recognised. the doha development round was certainly meant to be a development round. i think that the negotiating text on the table today shows that, if we conclude this round, it would indeed deliver on development. for instance, the market-opening obligations for developing countries will not be the same as for the developed countries, and the poorest countries – the least-developed countries – will not have to open their markets at all. the european union has supported this flexible approach. today the european union is the only industrialised bloc that has completely opened its markets for the least developed countries with duty-free, quota-free access in all areas. no other industrialised countries have done as much as we have. but these blocs will be forced to do the same as we have if we can conclude this round. regarding whether the agreement is good enough; the lisbon treaty, mr president, has a specific spirit and sets new limits for cooperation between the institutions of the european union. under this new system, parliament's role in individual rights matters is enhanced by the introduction of the codecision procedure. i therefore find it a little paradoxical that today, in the first plenary session after the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon, we should be obliged to discuss, including legal provisions, the limits of our new role before we even begin to operate and adapt to it. we all, without exception, agree on the need to take decisive action against terrorism. in this endeavour, strict measures are often necessary, such as the freezing of bank accounts and capital movements. on the other hand, respect for and protection of individual rights is indeed a fundamental value and core of the european union, and we cannot afford to ignore this when taking measures to ensure the safety of citizens, without, of course, this being a sign that we are not determined to give priority to the fight against terrorism and other criminal acts. the opinion of parliament's legal service and the case law of the court of justice of the european communities are certainly moving in this direction. we are directly elected by the citizens of the european union and that is precisely why we have a special responsibility to explain to the citizens that we are on the one hand guaranteeing their security and on the other hand fighting at the same time for the protection of their rights. we're the ones most responsible for this project.

your honor, i agree with the court that it is wrong to equate the presence of the crucifix in schoolrooms with disrespect for the religious beliefs of others. the issue we are discussing today is more social than legal. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the presence of religious symbols is not an indication of religious discrimination or coercion. it is a question of the tradition and history of each country, and often of its constitution, as is the case in my own country, greece. in greece, we have religious images in schoolrooms, not to impose a particular religion on students, but because it is part of our tradition, directly connected to the values and structures of our society, directly connected to the persecution and religious oppression for four centuries by the ottoman empire.
